, is an enhancement of this equivalence: the category of free
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodules for
G^{\vee}
is equivalent as monoidal categories to the category of semi-simple complexes in
D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)
. The second result, discussed in Section
6.2
, is an application of the first: the quantum Toda lattice of
G^{\vee}
is isomorphic as rings to the
G\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant homology of
\mathcal{G}r
.

We also prove a computational result on the isomorphism from the second result and discuss its semi-classical limit, which will be done in Section
6.3
and Section
6.4
respectively.

6.1.
Enhancement of geometric Satake equivalence

Let
\rho
be the half-sum of the positive roots. We grade
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
by requiring
\hslash
to have degree 2; every element of
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\subset U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
to have degree 2; and every element of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\subset U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
(
\alpha\in R
) to have degree
2\rho(\alpha^{\vee})+2
.

Definition 6.1
.

An
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule

M
is a graded left
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{op}
-module equipped with a linear algebraic
G^{\vee}
-action satisfying

(1)

\sigma(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\cdot M_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
and
\sigma(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}})\cdot M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+2\rho(\alpha^{\vee})}
for any
\alpha\in R
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\sigma:\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rightarrow\eendo_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the linearization of the
G^{\vee}
-action and
M_{i}
is the
i
-th graded piece of
M
;

(2)

g\cdot((x\otimes y)\cdot m)=((g\cdot x)\otimes(g\cdot y))\cdot(g\cdot m)
for any
m\in M
,
x,y\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
and
g\in G^{\vee}
; and

(3)

(x\otimes 1-1\otimes x)\cdot m=\hslash\sigma(x)m
for any
m\in M
and
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
.

Example 6.2
.

Let
V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be a finite dimensional graded
G^{\vee}
-module satisfying
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\cdot V_{i}\subseteq V_{i}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\cdot V_{i}\subseteq V_{i+2\rho(\alpha^{\vee})}
for any
\alpha\in R
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. (Up to a degree shift every irreducible representation has a unique such grading.) Define
\fr(V):=U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes V
and a left
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{op}
-module structure on
\fr(V)
by the formula

(x\otimes y)\cdot(z\otimes v):=xzy\otimes v-\hslash xz\otimes(y\cdot v),\quad v\in V,~x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},~z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}).

Then
\fr(V)
is an
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule. We call bimodules of this form
free
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodules
.

Remark 6.3
.

Similarly, we define an
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structure on
V\otimes U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
by the formula

(x\otimes y)\cdot(v\otimes z):=v\otimes xzy+\hslash(x\cdot v)\otimes zy,\quad v\in V,~x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},~z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}).

However, we obtain nothing new because it is just isomorphic to
\fr(V)
. The isomorphism is constructed by “moving” every factor
z\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\subset U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
from right to left using the exchange relation
v\otimes z\mapsto z\otimes v-\hslash\otimes(z\cdot v)
.

Example 6.4
.

Let
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
be the ring of
\hslash
-differential operators on
G^{\vee}
. Define a grading on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
as follows. Consider a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
G^{\vee}
defined by
c\cdot g:=(2\rho(c))g(2\rho(c))^{-1}
. Regular functions which have weight
m_{1}
with respect to this
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action are required to have degree
m_{1}
and vector fields which have weight
m_{2}
are required to have degree
m_{2}+2
. For any
x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
, denote by
x^{L}
(resp.
x^{R}
) the left-invariant (resp. right-invariant) vector field on
G^{\vee}
generated by
x
. Define two left
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{op}
-module structures
\cdot^{L}
and
\cdot^{R}
on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
:

(x\otimes 1)\cdot^{L}z:=x^{L}z~\text{ and }~(1\otimes x)\cdot^{L}z:=zx^{L},\quad x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},~z\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}

and

(x\otimes 1)\cdot^{R}z:=-x^{R}z~\text{ and }~(1\otimes x)\cdot^{R}z:=-zx^{R},\quad x\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},~z\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}.

Then
\cdot^{L}
and
\cdot^{R}
define two
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structures on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
, where the right and left translations are used for the corresponding
G^{\vee}
-action respectively. These two structures commute with each other in the obvious sense.

Definition 6.5
.

(1)

Define
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
to be the category of
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodules where the morphism spaces consist of graded
G^{\vee}
-equivariant
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{op}
-linear maps of degree zero.

(2)

Define
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
to be the full subcategory of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
consisting of free
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodules defined in Example
6.2
.

(3)

Define
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}
to be the full subcategory of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
consisting of objects which are quotients of objects of
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
. Define

M_{1}*M_{2}:=M_{1}\otimes_{U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}M_{2}

where the right (resp. left)
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module structure on
M_{1}
(resp.
M_{2}
) for the tensor product is the one induced by the given
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structure via the map
x\mapsto 1\otimes x
(resp.
x\mapsto x\otimes 1
). Clearly
M_{1}*M_{2}
has a natural
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structure and lies in
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
or
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}
if both
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
do. Moreover,
*
makes
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
,
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}
into monoidal categories.

Take a character
\chi_{0}:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
which is non-zero on each direct summand
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}}
. Let
M\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
. Denote by
\mathcal{I}^{M}_{-,\chi_{0}}
, or simply
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi_{0}}
if no confusion arises, the sub-
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-module of
M
generated by elements of the form
(1\otimes(x-\chi_{0}(x)))\cdot m
where
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
and
m\in M
. It is graded and preserved by
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
and
U^{\vee}_{-}
. Since
U^{\vee}_{-}
fixes every element of
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
,
(M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi_{0}})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
is naturally a
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module. Denote by
\mathcal{I}^{M}_{+}
, or simply
\mathcal{I}_{+}
, the sub-
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-module of
M
generated by elements of the form
(x\otimes 1)\cdot m
where
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}
and
m\in M
. It is graded and preserved by
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
. For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
, define an automorphism
\twist_{\lambda}
of the
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebra
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
by

\twist_{\lambda}(x):=x+\hslash\lambda(x),\quad x\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}.

For a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module
N
, denote by
\twist_{\lambda}(N)
the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module
N
with module structure twisted by
\twist_{\lambda}
.

Definition 6.7
.

(1)

(Kostant functor) Define an additive functor

\kappa_{\hslash}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}\rightarrow\left\{\text{graded }Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\text{-modules}\right\}

by

\kappa_{\hslash}(M):=(M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi_{0}})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}.

(2)

(Desymmetrized Kostant functor) Define an additive functor

\delta_{\hslash}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}\rightarrow\left\{\text{graded }U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\text{-modules}\right\}

by

\delta_{\hslash}(M):=\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}(M/(\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi_{0}}+\mathcal{I}_{+}))

where
\rho^{\vee}
is the half-sum of the positive coroots.

Remark 6.8
.

Up to natural equivalence, the (resp. desymmetrized) Kostant functor is independent of the choice of
\chi_{0}
. Indeed, for different choices of
\chi_{0}
, the multiplication by a suitable element of
T^{\vee}
induces a natural equivalence between the corresponding (resp. desymmetrized) Kostant functors. Unless otherwise specified, we will take
\chi_{0}
to be the character
\chi
defined in (
4.1
).

The functors
\kappa_{\hslash}
and
\delta_{\hslash}
are related as follows. Let
M\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
. The composition

\kappa_{\hslash}(M)=(M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}\hookrightarrow M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}\rightarrow M/(\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}+\mathcal{I}_{+})=\delta_{\hslash}(M)

is a homomorphism of modules with respect to the ring map

\Theta_{HC}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}\id_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}:Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\rightarrow U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})

where
\Theta_{HC}
is the
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra homomorphism from Section
2.2
. By extension of scalars, we obtain a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-linear map

(6.1)

\theta_{M}:U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\delta_{\hslash}(M).

Lemma 6.9
.

(
[
6
, Lemma 5]
) If
M\in\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
, then
\theta_{M}
is an isomorphism.
\square

Remark 6.10
.

See Appendix
C
for more discussions on Lemma
6.9
.

Define
\mathcal{R}
to be the sub-
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-algebra of
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})[\hslash^{-1}]
generated by
\hslash^{-1}(x\otimes 1-1\otimes x)
,
x\in Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
. Let
M\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
. Suppose
\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
is
\hslash
-torsion-free. (It is the case for
M\in\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
.) Then
\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
is naturally a graded
\mathcal{R}
-module.

Let
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}
be the full subcategory of
D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)
consisting of objects which are direct sums of degree shifts of simple objects of
\mathcal{P}_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)
. Put
\widehat{G}:=G\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Consider the
\widehat{G}
-equivariant hypercohomology functor
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}
on
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}
. A priori, it lands in the category of graded
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)
-modules. Observe that
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)
is naturally a graded algebra over
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\otimes H_{G}^{\bullet}(\pt)\simeq H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
. (The first factor corresponds to the canonical
\widehat{G}
-action on
\mathcal{G}r
, and the second corresponds to the canonical
G
-torsor on
\mathcal{G}r
.) Identify
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
with
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
via the
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra homomorphism
\Theta_{HC}
(see Section
2.2
) and the canonical isomorphism
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\simeq H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
so that
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)
is now a
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-algebra. One can show that
\hslash^{-1}(x\otimes 1-1\otimes x)\cdot 1
exists in
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)
for any
x\in Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
. This gives rise to a graded homomorphism

\phi:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)

of
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-algebras.

Theorem 6.11
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 1]
)
\phi
is bijective.
\square

Now, we state the first result mentioned at the beginning of Section
6
.

Theorem 6.12
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 2]
) There is a unique functor

\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}:\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}

such that

(1)

\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}
commutes with the degree shift functors;

(2)

for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
,
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(\fr(S(\lambda)))
is the
G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant intersection complex of
\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}
(
S(\lambda)
is graded such that every element of
S(\lambda)_{\mu}
has degree
2\rho(\mu)
); and

(3)

the diagram

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}
\{\text{gr. }\mathcal{R}\text{-mod}\}
\{\text{gr. }H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)\text{-mod}\}
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}
\kappa_{\hslash}|_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}}
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}|_{\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}}
\sim

is commutative, where the bottom isomorphism is induced by
\phi
from Theorem
6.11
.

Moreover,
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}
is an equivalence of monoidal categories and the natural equivalence connecting the composite functors in the above diagram is compatible with the monoidal structures on these functors.
\square

6.2.
Quantum Toda lattice and equivariant homology of affine Grassmannian

Recall the functors
\kappa_{\hslash}
and
\delta_{\hslash}
defined in Definition
6.7
and the two
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structures
\cdot^{L}
and
\cdot^{R}
on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
defined in Example
6.4
.

Definition 6.13
.

Define

\mathcal{K}:=\kappa_{\hslash}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}})

where we apply
\kappa_{\hslash}
to
\cdot^{R}
.

Since these two
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structures commute with each other, it follows that
\cdot^{L}
induces an
\hslash
-Harish-Chandra bimodule structure on
\mathcal{K}
.

Definition 6.14
.

(1)

The graded
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is called the
quantum Toda lattice
.

(2)

The graded
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is called the
desymmetrized quantum Toda lattice
.

The ring structure on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
induces an algebra structure on
\mathcal{K}
, i.e. a morphism
\mathcal{K}*\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
satisfying the unitality and associativity axioms. Since
\kappa_{\hslash}
is a monoidal functor, it follows that
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is naturally a graded
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebra and
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is naturally a graded right
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
-module.

On the other hand, define

\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r}:=\underset{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}}{\varinjlim}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}}

to be the filtered colimit of the dualizing complexes
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}}
of
\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
). It has an algebra structure given by convolution so that
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r})
is a graded
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebra. Notice that

H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r})\simeq H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)

as graded
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebras where the algebra structure on the RHS is the one defined in Section
2.1
.

Now, we state the second result mentioned at the beginning of Section
6
.

Theorem 6.15
.

(
[
6
, Section 6.4]
)

(1)

There exists a cohomological functor

F:D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}

such that

(i)

F
extends
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}^{-1}
(see Theorem
6.12
for the definition of
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}
);

(ii)

for any
X,Y\in D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)
with
X\in\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}
, the canonical map

F_{X,Y}:\Hom_{D_{G(\mathcal{O})\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}}(\mathcal{G}r)}(X,Y)\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}}(F(X),F(Y))

is bijective;

(iii)

F
is quasi-monoidal in the sense that there exist a morphism
\text{unit}\rightarrow F(\text{unit})
and for each pair
(X,Y)
of objects of the source category a morphism
F(X)*F(Y)\rightarrow F(X*Y)
such that these morphisms are functorial and satisfy the unitality and associativity axioms;

(iv)

the quasi-monoidal structure on
F
from (iii) extends the monoidal structure on
\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}^{-1}
from Theorem
6.12
; and

(v)

\kappa_{\hslash}\circ F\simeq H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}
as quasi-monoidal functors.

(2)

The functor
F
from (1) sends
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r}
to
\mathcal{K}
in the sense that
\mathcal{K}
represents the functor
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}\ni M\mapsto\underset{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}}{\varinjlim}\Hom_{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}_{\hslash}}(M,F(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}}))
. Moreover, it induces a graded isomorphism

\Theta^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{K}}:\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\simeq\underset{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}}{\varinjlim}\kappa_{\hslash}\circ F(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}})\simeq\underset{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}}{\varinjlim}H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}})\simeq H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)

of
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebras and of
\mathcal{R}
-modules.

\square

6.3.
A computational result

Definition 6.16
.

Define

\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}:=\Theta^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{K}}:\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)

where
\Theta^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{K}}
comes from Theorem
6.15
(2), and

\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r)

to be the map obtained from
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
by extension of scalars with respect to the canonical map
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\simeq H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\rightarrow U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\simeq H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
. (We have
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\simeq U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
by Lemma
C.1
.)

In this subsection, we prove a computational result about
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
. Recall we have been using
\chi
to define
\kappa_{\hslash}
. See Remark
6.8
. But we would like to make an exception: instead of
\chi
, we use
-\chi
for
\kappa_{\hslash}
applied to
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
when we define
\mathcal{K}
. Although changing the character does not change the isomorphism class of algebra objects of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
represented by
\mathcal{K}
, it does change how
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta/\kappa}
will look. Our choice will make Proposition
6.17
below hold. Notice that we will still use
\chi
for
\kappa_{\hslash}
(resp.
\delta_{\hslash}
) applied to
\mathcal{K}
when we define the (resp. desymmetrized) quantum Toda lattices.

Let
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
\mu\in Q^{\vee}
. Recall an MV cycle of type
\lambda
and weight
\mu
is any irreducible component of
\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}
of dimension
\rho(\lambda-\mu)
where

S^{-}_{\mu}:=\left\{y\in\mathcal{G}r\left|~\lim_{s\to\infty}2\rho^{\vee}(s)\cdot y=t^{\mu}\right.\right\}

and
\rho
(resp.
\rho^{\vee}
) is the half-sum of the positive roots (resp. coroots). By
[
39
, Proposition 13.1]
,
\gs(S(\lambda))
is the costandard sheaf
{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{0}((\iota_{\lambda})_{*}\mathbb{C}[2\rho(\lambda)])
where
\iota_{\lambda}:\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}r
is the inclusion (recall
S(\lambda):=H^{0}(G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-};G^{\vee}\times^{B_{-}^{\vee}}\mathbb{C}_{\lambda})
). There are canonical isomorphisms

(6.2)

S(\lambda)_{\mu}\simeq H_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}^{2\rho(\mu)}(\gs(S(\lambda)))\simeq H_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}^{2\rho(\mu)}((\iota_{\lambda})_{*}\mathbb{C}[2\rho(\lambda)])\simeq\mathbb{C}\langle\text{MV cycles of type }\lambda\text{ and weight }\mu\rangle

where the first isomorphism is part of the geometric Satake equivalence, the second is induced by the canonical morphism
\gs(S(\lambda))\rightarrow(\iota_{\lambda})_{*}\mathbb{C}[2\rho(\lambda)]
, and the third follows from base-change and excision (see
[
39
, Proposition 3.10]
and also
[
2
, Proposition 11.1]
).

Let
v_{\lambda}^{*}\in S(\lambda)^{*}
be the unique vector satisfying
v_{\lambda}^{*}|_{S(\lambda)_{\neq\lambda}}\equiv 0
and
\langle v_{\lambda}^{*},v_{e}\rangle=1
where
v_{e}\in S(\lambda)_{\lambda}\simeq\mathbb{C}
is the vector corresponding to the unique MV cycle
\{t^{\lambda}\}
of type
\lambda
and weight
\lambda
via the composite isomorphism (
6.2
). For any
v\in S(\lambda)
, denote by
f_{\lambda,v}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
the regular function
g\mapsto\langle v_{\lambda}^{*},g\cdot v\rangle
. Observe that
f_{\lambda,v}(u\cdot-)=f_{\lambda,v}(-)
for any
u\in U^{\vee}_{-}
. It follows that
f_{\lambda,v}
represents an element of
\mathcal{K}
and hence an element of
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
. By abuse of notation, both of these elements will be denoted by
[f_{\lambda,v}]
.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
,
\mu\in Q^{\vee}
and
v\in S(\lambda)_{\mu}
. Suppose
v
corresponds to an MV cycle
Z
(of type
\lambda
and weight
\mu
) via the composite isomorphism (
6.2
). Then

\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v}])=[Z]\in H_{2\rho(\lambda-\mu)}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r).

Before proving Proposition
6.17
, let us go back to Theorem
6.12
in Section
6.1
. A key step of the proof given in
[
6
]
is to construct for each
V\in\rep G^{\vee}
an isomorphism

\Theta^{\kappa}_{\fr(V)}:\kappa_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))

of
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-modules. This amounts to constructing a
W
-equivariant isomorphism

\Theta^{\delta}_{\fr(V)}:\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))

of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-modules. Consider the filtrations

\{F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\}_{\mu\in Q^{\vee}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\{F^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))\}_{\mu\in Q^{\vee}}

on
\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))
and
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))
respectively, defined by

F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V)):=\fr(V)_{\geqslant\mu}/\left(\fr(V)_{\geqslant\mu}\cap(\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}+\mathcal{I}_{+})\right)

where
\fr(V)_{\geqslant\mu}:=V_{\geqslant\mu}\otimes U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
(recall
\fr(V)\simeq V\otimes U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
by Remark
6.3
), and

F^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V)):=\im\left(H_{\overline{S_{\mu}^{-}},\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))\rightarrow H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))\right)

where the map in the RHS is induced by the inclusion
\overline{S_{\mu}^{-}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}r
. By
[
6
, Lemma 1 & Lemma 6]
, there are canonical isomorphisms of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-modules

grF^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\otimes V_{\mu}~\text{ and }~grF^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mu}\otimes H_{S^{-}_{\mu}}^{2\rho(\mu)}(\gs(V))

where
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}:=U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
on which
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
and
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
act via the identity and the ring homomorphism
z\mapsto\twist_{-\mu}(\Theta_{HC}(z))
respectively. By the geometric Satake equivalence, we thus have an isomorphism

(6.3)

grF^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\simeq grF^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))

of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-modules.

Theorem 6.18
.

(
[
6
, Theorem 6]
) There exists a unique isomorphism

\Theta^{\delta}_{\fr(V)}:\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))

of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-modules such that

(1)

it is compatible with the above filtrations, i.e.
\Theta^{\delta}_{\fr(V)}\left(F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(V))\right)\subseteq F^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(V))
for any
\mu\in Q^{\vee}
; and

(2)

it induces isomorphism (
6.3
) on the associated graded pieces.

Moreover,
\Theta^{\delta}_{\fr(V)}
is
W
-equivariant and hence induces the desired isomorphism
\Theta^{\kappa}_{\fr(V)}
.

\square

Proof of Proposition
6.17
.

Observe that the assignment
v\mapsto[f_{\lambda,v}]
defines a morphism

f_{\lambda}:\fr(S(\lambda))[2\rho(\lambda)]\rightarrow\mathcal{K}

in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
. (Here,
S(\lambda)
is graded such that every element of
S(\lambda)_{\mu}
has degree
2\rho(\mu)
.) Let
g_{\lambda}:\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(S(\lambda))[2\rho(\lambda)]\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r}
be the morphism induced by
f_{\lambda}
via Theorem
6.15
. Put
M(\lambda):=\fr(S(\lambda))[2\rho(\lambda)]\in\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}^{fr}_{\hslash}
and
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}:=\mathbb{G}\mathbb{S}(S(\lambda))\in\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}
. We have the following commutative diagram

\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet+2\rho(\lambda)}(\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r)
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet+2\rho(\lambda)}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda})
F^{\mu}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet+2\rho(\lambda)}(\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})
F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))
\delta_{\hslash}(f_{\lambda})
~\Theta^{\delta}_{M(\lambda)}
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(g_{\lambda})
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda}))
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda})
\iota_{2}
j
\iota_{1}
~\Theta^{\delta}_{M(\lambda)}|_{F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))}

where
\iota_{1},\iota_{2}
are the inclusion maps,
\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}:\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}r
is the inclusion map and
j
is the canonical isomorphism.

By assumption, we have
[v\otimes 1]\in F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))
, and by definition, we have
[f_{\lambda,v}]=\delta_{\hslash}(f_{\lambda})\circ\iota_{1}([v\otimes 1])
. Hence, by the above diagram, we have

(6.4)

\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v}])=H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda})\circ H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda}))(x)

where
x:=j^{-1}\circ\Theta^{\delta}_{M(\lambda)}|_{F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))}([v\otimes 1])
. Observe that
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda}))(x)
lies in
H_{2\rho(\lambda-\mu)}^{\widehat{T}}(\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda})
and
2\rho(\lambda-\mu)
is the maximal real dimension of the irreducible components of
\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}
, by
[
39
, Theorem 3.2]
. It follows that
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v}])
is equal to a linear combination, with
complex
coefficients, of the
\widehat{T}
-equivariant fundamental classes of the irreducible components of
\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}
of maximal dimension, i.e. the MV cycles of type
\lambda
and weight
\mu
. Since these coefficients are constant polynomials in the equivariant parameters, we can determine them by looking at the non-equivariant case.

Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{eq=0}
the functor killing all equivariant parameters. By condition (2) in Theorem
6.18
, the element

x^{\prime}:=\mathcal{F}_{eq=0}(j^{-1}\circ\Theta^{\delta}_{M(\lambda)}|_{F^{\mu}\delta_{\hslash}(M(\lambda))})([v\otimes 1])\in H^{2\rho(\mu)}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})\simeq H^{2\rho(\mu)}_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}(\mathcal{J}_{\lambda})

corresponds to
v\in S(\lambda)_{\mu}
via the first isomorphism in (
6.2
). Denote by
\iota_{\lambda}
and
\iota_{\mu,\lambda}
the inclusions
\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}r
and
\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}r
respectively. Recall there is a canonical morphism
\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}\rightarrow(\iota_{\lambda})_{*}\mathbb{C}[2\rho(\lambda)]
which we shall denote by
h_{\lambda}
. Observe that
\iota_{\lambda}^{!}(h_{\lambda})
is an isomorphism and

\iota_{\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda})\circ\iota_{\lambda}^{!}(h_{\lambda})^{-1}[2\rho(\lambda)]:\mathbb{C}[4\rho(\lambda)]\simeq\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}}

is equal to
c_{\lambda}\id_{\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{G}r^{\lambda}}}
for some
c_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}
. It is not difficult to see that the composition

\displaystyle H_{2\rho(\lambda-\mu)}(\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda})

\displaystyle\simeq H_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}^{2\rho(\mu-\lambda)}((\iota_{\lambda})_{*}\mathbb{C}[4\rho(\lambda)])\xrightarrow{a^{-1}}H_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}^{2\rho(\mu-\lambda)}(\mathcal{J}_{\lambda}[2\rho(\lambda)])

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{b}H^{2\rho(\mu-\lambda)}(\mathbb{D}_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}})\simeq H_{2\rho(\lambda-\mu)}(\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}\cap\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda})

is equal to
c_{\lambda}
times the identity, where

a:=H_{\overline{S^{-}_{\mu}}}^{2\rho(\mu-\lambda)}(h_{\lambda}[2\rho(\lambda)])\quad\text{ and }\quad b:=H^{2\rho(\mu-\lambda)}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda})).

(Notice that
a
is the second isomorphism in (
6.2
).) Therefore,
H^{\bullet}(\iota_{\mu,\leqslant\lambda}^{!}(g_{\lambda}))
sends
x^{\prime}
to
c_{\lambda}[Z]
.

It remains to show
c_{\lambda}=1
. Notice that
c_{\lambda}
depends only on
\lambda
but not
\mu
. By Lemma
6.19
which we will prove in the next subsection,
\mathcal{F}_{eq=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z}_{0})
where
\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\{b\in B^{\vee}|~b\cdot e=e\}
. Since
G^{\vee}
is of adjoint type, we have
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq U^{\vee}
. Now take
\mu=\lambda
and
v=v_{e}\in S(\lambda)_{\lambda}
, the vector corresponding to the unique MV cycle
\{t^{\lambda}\}
of type
\lambda
and weight
\lambda
. Notice that
f_{\lambda,v_{e}}|_{U^{\vee}}\equiv 1
. Hence,

[f_{\lambda,v_{e}}]=1\in\mathcal{F}_{eq=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})).

Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}|_{\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})}
is a ring homomorphism, we have
\mathcal{F}_{eq=0}(\Phi_{BF}^{\delta})(1)=1
. But we also have
[t^{\lambda}]=1\in H_{0}(\mathcal{G}r)
. Therefore, we have
c_{\lambda}=1
, as desired.

\square

6.4.
Semi-classical limit

In this subsection, we discuss the semi-classical limit of the homomorphism
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
defined in Definition
6.16
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
denote the functor
N\rightarrow N/\hslash N
.

Lemma 6.19
.

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))
is isomorphic as
\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-modules to the coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z})
of the scheme

\mathcal{Z}:=\left\{(b,\xi)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi\right.\right\}

where
e
is defined in (
4.2
) and the
\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure on
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z})
is induced by the morphism
\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\spec\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\simeq(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})^{*}
defined by
(b,\xi)\mapsto\beta(\xi,-)|_{\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}}
. (
\beta
is also defined in (
4.2
).)

Proof.

We first determine
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\mathcal{K})
. By Definition
6.13
, we have
\mathcal{K}=\kappa_{\hslash}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}})
where
\kappa_{\hslash}
is applied to
\cdot^{R}
defined in Example
6.4
. By Lemma
C.2
which says that
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
commutes with
\kappa_{\hslash}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\mathcal{K})
is isomorphic to
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}))\simeq\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{O}(T^{*}G^{\vee}))
. By the definition of
\cdot^{R}
and the assumption that the character
-\chi
is used for
\kappa_{\hslash}
(see the paragraph following Definition
6.16
), we see that the last module is equal to
(\mathcal{O}(T^{*}G^{\vee})/\mathcal{I}_{1})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
where
\mathcal{I}_{1}
is the ideal generated by
-x^{R}+\chi(x)
(
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
) and the
U^{\vee}_{-}
-action is induced by left translations on
G^{\vee}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\mathcal{K})
is isomorphic to the coordinate ring of
Y:=U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus\widetilde{Y}
, the left
U^{\vee}_{-}
-quotient of the closed subscheme
\widetilde{Y}
of
T^{*}G^{\vee}
defined by the equations
x^{R}-\chi(x)
(
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
).

We now determine
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))
. Clearly,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
commutes with
\delta_{\hslash}
, and so, by the previous paragraph,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))
is isomorphic to the coordinate ring of certain closed subscheme
Y^{\prime}
of
Y
which can be expressed as
U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}
for some closed subscheme
\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{Y}
. It is straightforward to see that

\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}=\left\{(g,\xi)\in G^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\left|~\xi\in e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee},~g\cdot\xi\in e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}.

(Here, we have identified
T^{*}G^{\vee}
, first with
G^{\vee}\times(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
via left translations, and then with
G^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
via
\beta
.) But by Kostant’s slice theorem, we have, for any
\xi\in e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
,

\left\{g\in G^{\vee}\left|~g\cdot\xi\in e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}=U^{\vee}_{-}\cdot\left\{b\in B^{\vee}\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi\right.\right\},

and hence

\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}=U^{\vee}_{-}\cdot\left\{(b,\xi)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi\right.\right\}=U^{\vee}_{-}\cdot\mathcal{Z}.

Therefore,

Y^{\prime}\simeq U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}\simeq U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus\left(U^{\vee}_{-}\cdot\mathcal{Z}\right)\simeq\mathcal{Z}.

∎

Recall
Yun-Zhu’s isomorphism

[
53
]

\Phi_{YZ}:\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{-\bullet}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r)

where

(6.5)

B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}:=\{(b,h)\in B^{\vee}\times\spec H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt)|~b\cdot e^{T}(h)=e^{T}(h)\}

is the centralizer group scheme of the
H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt)
-point
e^{T}
of
\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}
defined by
e^{T}:=e+f
with
e:=\sum_{i=1}^{r}|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}e_{i}^{\vee}
(see (
4.2
)) and
f:\alpha^{\vee}_{i}\mapsto|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\alpha_{i}
. (This map is also constructed by Bezrukavnikov, Finkelberg and Mirković
[
7
]
.) Clearly, the isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{ccc}B^{\vee}\times\spec H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt)&\rightarrow&B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\\[5.0pt]
(b,h)&\mapsto&(b,e^{T}(h))\end{array}

induces an isomorphism
B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{Z}
of schemes over
\spec H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt)\simeq(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})^{*}
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is defined in Lemma
6.19
. (Notice that
\beta(e+f(h),-)|_{\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}}=h
for any
h\in(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})^{*}
.)

Proposition 6.20
.

After identifying
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))
with
\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})
via Lemma
6.19
and the above isomorphism, we have
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{BF}^{\delta})=\Phi_{YZ}
.

Proof.

There is a
W
-action on
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))
which is induced by the isomorphism
\theta_{\mathcal{K}}
(see Lemma
C.1
). There is also a
W
-action on
\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})
because
B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}\simeq\mathcal{Z}
is the base change of the
universal centralizer

\mathcal{C}:=G^{\vee}\setminus\!\!\!\setminus\{(g,\xi)\in G^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}|~g\cdot\xi=\xi\}\simeq U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus\{(g,\xi)\in G^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-})|~g\cdot\xi=\xi\}

along the canonical morphism
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\simeq e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\rightarrow U^{\vee}_{-}\setminus(e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-})\simeq\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}/W
. It is not difficult to see that these two actions coincide under our identification (because
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C})
which can be proved the same way how Lemma
6.19
is proved). By definition,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{BF}^{\delta})
is
W
-equivariant, and by the proof of
[
53
, Proposition 6.6]
,
\Phi_{YZ}
is
W
-equivariant as well.

Let
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
. Denote by
v_{e}\in S(\lambda)_{\lambda}
the vector corresponding to the unique MV cycle
\{t^{\lambda}\}
of type
\lambda
and weight
\lambda
via the composite isomorphism (
6.2
). By
[
53
, Remark 3.4]
, we have

\Phi_{YZ}([f_{\lambda,v_{e}}])=[t^{\lambda}]\in H_{0}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r).

Since
\Phi_{YZ}
is a
W
-equivariant isomorphism and
[t^{\lambda}]
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) together with their
W
-translates generate the module
H_{\bullet}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r)
over
\fof(H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt))
, it follows that
[f_{\lambda,v_{e}}]
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) together with their
W
-translates generate the module
\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})
over
\fof(H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt))
. Therefore, we are done if we can show

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{BF}^{\delta})([f_{\lambda,v_{e}}])=[t^{\lambda}]

for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
. But this is a special case of Proposition
6.17
.
∎

7.
Proof of main results

7.1.
Summary

We construct
\Phi_{mir}
as stated in Theorem
1.2
by completing the following diagram.

G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
\Phi_{0}
\Phi_{1~}~
~\Phi_{2}
\Phi_{3}

Here,

(1)

\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is the desymmetrized quantum Toda lattice defined in Definition
6.14
.

(2)

\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
are some submodules which we will define in Section
7.2
and Section
7.3
respectively.

(3)

\Phi_{0}
is a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map which we will define in Section
7.4
. We will show that it is bijective (Proposition
7.24
).

(4)

\Phi_{1}
is a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map which we will define in Section
7.5
. We will show that it is surjective (Proposition
7.30
) and becomes bijective after applying the functor
N\mapsto N/\hslash N
(Proposition
7.31
).

(5)

\Phi_{2}
is a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map which we will define in Section
7.6
. We will show that it is surjective (Proposition
7.38
) and becomes bijective after applying the functor
N\mapsto N/\hslash N
(Proposition
7.39
).

(6)

\Phi_{3}
is the unique
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map such that
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
. We will show that it exists (Proposition
7.41
) and is bijective (Proposition
7.44
) in Section
7.7
.

We define

\Phi_{mir}:=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{0}

which is thus a bijective
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map. We will show that it is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear (Proposition
7.46
) in Section
7.8
. In Section
7.9
, we will verify the rest of the properties of
\Phi_{mir}
as well as other statements in Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4
.

Remark 7.1
.

Readers who have read Section
1.2
may find the following dictionary useful:

\widetilde{\Phi}_{R}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{0}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ q|_{\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})},\quad\Phi_{YZ}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{BF}^{\delta},\quad\Phi_{PLS}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{GMP}^{T}

and

\Phi_{2}\circ q|_{\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})}=\Phi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}

where
q:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
is the quotient map and
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
(resp.
\Phi_{GMP}^{T}
) comes from Definition
6.16
(resp. Definition
5.11
).

7.2.
The submodule
\mathcal{V}

Let
\chi:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the character from (
4.1
).

Definition 7.2
.

Define
\mathcal{V}\subset D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
to be the sum of

(1)

the left ideal generated by
x^{L}-\chi(x)
with
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
;

(2)

the left ideal generated by
x^{R}-\chi(x)
with
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
;

(3)

the right ideal generated by
x^{L}
with
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}
; and

(4)

the sub-
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module generated by
\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\simeq\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
satisfying
\varphi|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\equiv 0
where the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
is defined by left multiplication of left-invariant vector fields on
G^{\vee}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

\mathcal{V}
is homogeneous with respect to the natural grading on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
so there is a grading on the quotient
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

This is straightforward and left to the reader.
∎

Definition 7.4
.

Define a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure
\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}
on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
by the equality

x\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}y:=(x^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(x))y,\quad x\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee},~y\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))

where
\rho^{\vee}
is the half-sum of the positive coroots. It is not hard to see that
\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}
induces a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
.

Recall the paragraph before Proposition
6.17
where we defined, for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, the vector
v_{e}\in S(\lambda)_{\lambda}
and, for any
v\in S(\lambda)
, the regular function
f_{\lambda,v}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
.

Definition 7.5
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, define

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))

by

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}(y):=f_{\lambda,v_{e}}y\qquad y\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})).

It is
(-\lambda)
-twisted
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear in the sense of Definition
4.15
.

Lemma 7.6
.

(Shift operators)
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
descends to a
(-\lambda)
-twisted
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear map

\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}.

Proof.

We have to show
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
. Clearly
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
preserves the subspaces (1), (2) and (4) from Definition
7.2
. Let
y
be an element of the remaining subspace (3). We may assume
y=x^{L}y^{\prime}
for some
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}
and
y^{\prime}\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{x^{L}}f_{\lambda,v_{e}}=0
, it follows that

f_{\lambda,v_{e}}y=x^{L}f_{\lambda,v_{e}}y^{\prime}-\hslash(\mathcal{L}_{x^{L}}f_{\lambda,v_{e}})y^{\prime}=x^{L}f_{\lambda,v_{e}}y^{\prime}\equiv 0~(\bmod{\mathcal{V}}).

This gives
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}(y)\in\mathcal{V}
.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
is invertible.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}
is invertible (Lemma
4.18
) and two results which will be proved in Section
7.4
, namely, Lemma
7.23
and Proposition
7.24
.
∎

7.3.
The submodule
\mathcal{W}

Recall
\mathcal{K}:=\kappa_{\hslash}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}})
defined in Definition
6.13
.

Definition 7.8
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
v\in S(\lambda)
, define
a_{\lambda,v}\in\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
as follows. Fix a homogeneous
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})^{W}
-basis
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{|W|}\}
of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
with
z_{1}=1
. Notice that
z_{i}
has strictly positive degree for any
i\geqslant 2
. Recall the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear map
\theta_{\fr(S(\lambda))}:U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(\fr(S(\lambda)))\rightarrow\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(S(\lambda)))
. Since it is bijective (Lemma
6.9
), there exist unique
\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,1},\ldots,\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,|W|}\in\kappa_{\hslash}(\fr(S(\lambda)))
such that

(7.1)

[1\otimes v]=\sum_{i=1}^{|W|}\theta_{\fr(S(\lambda))}(z_{i}\otimes\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,i}).

Let
f_{\lambda}:\fr(S(\lambda))[2\rho(\lambda)]\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
be the morphism in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}
defined by
1\otimes v^{\prime}\mapsto[f_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}]
. (
f_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}
is defined in the paragraph before Proposition
6.17
.) We define

a_{\lambda,v}:=\kappa_{\hslash}(f_{\lambda})(\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,1}).

Lemma 7.9
.

Suppose
v\in S(\lambda)_{\mu}
for some
\mu\in Q^{\vee}
. Then
a_{\lambda,v}
is represented by
f_{\lambda,v}
plus an element which is equal to a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})
-linear combination of
f_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}
with
v^{\prime}\in S(\lambda)_{<\mu}
. Here, the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})
-module structure on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
is given by the left multiplication of left-invariant vector fields.

Proof.

By Lemma
C.1
applied to
M:=\fr(S(\lambda)_{\leqslant\mu})
, every
\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,i}
lies in the image of the canonical map
\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(\fr(S(\lambda)))
and hence is represented by a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})
-linear combination of
1\otimes v^{\prime\prime}
with
v^{\prime\prime}\in S(\lambda)_{\leqslant\mu}
. By a degree argument, we cannot have
v^{\prime\prime}\in S(\lambda)_{\mu}
if
i\geqslant 2
. The result now follows from (
7.1
) and the canonical isomorphism of
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-modules

\delta_{\hslash}(M)/\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(S(\lambda)_{<\mu}))\simeq\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}))\otimes S(\lambda)_{\mu}.

∎

Recall
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is naturally a right
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
-module. Hence
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
is a right
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module. For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
w\in W
, denote by
v_{w}\in S(\lambda)_{w(\lambda)}
the vector corresponding to the unique MV cycle of type
\lambda
and weight
w(\lambda)
via the composite isomorphism (
6.2
). (Notice that
v_{w}
depends on
\lambda
. But since it is unlikely to cause any confusion, we drop
\lambda
from the notation for simplicity.)

Definition 7.10
.

Define
\mathcal{W}\subset\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
to be the vector subspace generated by elements of the form
x\cdot a
where
x\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
and
a
is either

(1)

a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
for some
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
; or

(2)

a_{\lambda,v}
for some
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
.

Remark 7.11
.

The definition of
\mathcal{W}
is inspired by
[
10
]
. See Section 6.1 therein.

Lemma 7.12
.

\mathcal{W}
is homogeneous with respect to the natural grading on
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
so there is a grading on the quotient
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

This is straightforward and left to the reader.
∎

Definition 7.13
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, define

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))

by

(7.2)

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}([x]):=[f_{\lambda,v_{e}}x]\qquad[x]\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))

where
x\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
is any representative of
[x]
. (Notice that
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is a sub-quotient of
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
.) It is
(-\lambda)
-twisted
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear in the sense of Definition
4.15
.

Remark 7.14
.

One has to show that the RHS of (
7.2
) is well-defined, i.e.
f_{\lambda,v_{e}}x
represents an element of
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
which is independent of the representative
x
. Since the proof is straightforward, we omit it.

Lemma 7.15
.

(Shift operators)
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
descends to a
(-\lambda)
-twisted
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear map

\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}\rightarrow\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
is invertible.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by showing that (1)
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
is invertible and (2) its inverse preserves
\mathcal{W}
.

Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is an isomorphism of modules with respect to the isomorphism
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
of rings (Theorem
6.15
) and
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v_{e}}])=[t^{\lambda}]
(Proposition
6.17
),
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}|_{\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})}
is identified with
[t^{\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-
under this isomorphism. (A priori,
[t^{\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-
is a map from
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)
to
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r)
. But we can extend it to an endomorphism of
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r)
by extension of scalars because it is a homomorphism of modules with respect to the composition
H_{\widehat{G}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\hookrightarrow H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\xrightarrow{\twist_{-\lambda}}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
.) Clearly,
[t^{\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-
has an inverse which is given by
[t^{-\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-
. This proves (1).

Observe that the left multiplication by
[t^{-\lambda}]
commutes with the right multiplication by any elements of
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)
. Since
\mathcal{W}
is additively generated by
x\cdot a
where
x\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
is arbitrary and
a
belongs to a subset of
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
(see Definition
7.10
), (2) follows from this observation and the fact that
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is an isomorphism of modules with respect to
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
.
∎

7.4.
The map
\Phi_{0}

For any
\alpha\in R
, pick a generator
e_{\alpha^{\vee}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}}
. Denote by
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
(
\beta\in R
) and
\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}:G^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
the unique functions satisfying

(7.3)

e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}=\sum_{\beta\in R}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}+\sum_{i}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle h_{i}^{L}

where
\{h_{i}\}
and
\{h^{i}\}
are dual bases of
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
and
(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})^{*}
respectively. Consider the splitting
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
which induces a splitting of the tangent bundle
\mathcal{T}_{G^{\vee}}
of
G^{\vee}
by left translations. Let
\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}
and
\pr_{\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}}^{L}
denote the projections from
\mathcal{T}_{G^{\vee}}
onto the corresponding direct summands. Let
\alpha\in-R^{+}
. Define

\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}:=\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})\oplus 0\in\mathfrak{X}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})/Z(L^{\vee})).

Lemma 7.17
.

For any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
, we have

\displaystyle\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})\widetilde{\varphi}+\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\widetilde{\varphi}+\left(\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}})\right)\widetilde{\varphi}-\sum_{i}h_{i}\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle\widetilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}\left(-(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}))+\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}-\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}}))\right)+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{\varphi}~\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})).

(Recall
\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}
is defined in Definition
7.4
.)

Proof.

First by the equality
\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})=e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle-\widetilde{\varphi}\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}+\left(\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}})\right)\widetilde{\varphi}

(7.4)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}\left(-(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}))+\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}-\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}}))\right).

Next, using
\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})=\sum_{i}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle h_{i}^{L}+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}=~

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle h_{i}^{L}+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\right)\widetilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i}h_{i}^{L}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle-\hslash\sum_{i}\langle h^{i},\mathcal{L}_{h_{i}^{L}}\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}-\hslash\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}\mathcal{L}_{e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\right)\widetilde{\varphi}.

Observe that
\mathcal{L}_{h_{i}^{L}}\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}=0
and
\mathcal{L}_{e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}=\beta^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})
. It follows that

(7.5)

\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}=\sum_{i}h_{i}\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle\widetilde{\varphi}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})\widetilde{\varphi}+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{\varphi}.

Our result now follows from (
7.4
), (
7.5
) and the equality
\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}
.
∎

Recall the Rietsch mirror
(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p,\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}})
(Definition
4.2
). Let
\alpha\in-R^{+}
. Observe that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}
is tangent to
X^{\vee}_{P}
. Define

\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}:=\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\in\mathfrak{X}(X^{\vee}_{P}/Z(L^{\vee})).

Lemma 7.18
.

\{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}\}_{\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P})}
is a global frame of
X^{\vee}_{P}
relative to
Z(L^{\vee})
.

Proof.

This amounts to showing
\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\cap g^{-1}\cdot V=0
for any
g\in B^{\vee}\cap U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}Z(L^{\vee})U^{\vee}_{-}
where
V:=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P})}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\subset\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
. This follows from the facts that
U^{\vee}_{-}
preserves
V
,
\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0}
maps
V
into
\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}
and
B_{-}^{\vee}
preserves
\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
.
∎

Remark 7.19
.

Compare the frame
\{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}\}_{\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P})}
with the one introduced in
[
45
, Lemma 5.4]
.

We now define
\Phi_{0}
. Since the fiberwise volume form
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\in\Omega^{top}(X^{\vee}_{P}/Z(L^{\vee}))
is nowhere vanishing, we have

\Omega^{top}(X^{\vee}_{P}/Z(L^{\vee}))=\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})\cdot\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{top-1}(X^{\vee}_{P}/Z(L^{\vee}))=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P})}\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})\cdot\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}.

It is not hard to see that

G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\simeq M/M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}

where
M:=U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})
and
M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}\subseteq M
is the vector subspace generated by elements of the form

\displaystyle m_{\alpha}(z,\varphi)

\displaystyle:=~

\displaystyle z\otimes\left(\hslash\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}\right)\varphi+\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\varphi+(\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}W)\varphi\right)-\sum_{i}zh_{i}\otimes(\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}p^{*}\langle h^{i},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle)\varphi

where
\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+})
,
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})
. Notice that
m_{\alpha}(z,\varphi)
is in fact well-defined for any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
and also contained in
M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}
.

Definition 7.20
.

Define a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}:M\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}

by

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}(z\otimes\varphi):=[z\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}]\qquad z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}),~\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})

where
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
is any extension of
\varphi
and
\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}
is defined in Definition
7.4
. (It is well-defined because
\mathcal{V}
contains the subspace (4) from Definition
7.2
.)

Lemma 7.21
.

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}
descends to a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map

\Phi_{0}:M/M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}\simeq G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}.

Proof.

We have to show
\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}(m_{\alpha}(z,\varphi))=0
for any
\alpha\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+})
,
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})
. By Lemma
A.1
, Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.3
,

\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}),\quad\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}})\quad\text{and}\quad\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle

are extensions of

\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}},\quad\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}W\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}p^{*}\langle h^{i},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle

respectively. It follows that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}(m_{\alpha}(z,\varphi))
is represented by

z\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\left(\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})\widetilde{\varphi}+\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{\zeta}_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\widetilde{\varphi}+\left(\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}})\right)\widetilde{\varphi}-\sum_{i}h_{i}\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle\widetilde{\varphi}\right)

where
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
is an extension of
\varphi
. The result now follows from Lemma
7.17
.
∎

Lemma 7.22
.

\Phi_{0}
is graded.

Proof.

This is because
[\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}]
has degree 0 (Lemma
4.14
).
∎

Lemma 7.23
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, we have

\Phi_{0}\circ\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{0}

where
\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
come from Lemma
4.17
and Lemma
7.6
respectively.

Proof.

It suffices to note that
f_{\lambda,v_{e}}
(regarded as a regular function on
G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})
) is an extension of
\lambda\circ p\in\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P})
.
∎

Proposition 7.24
.

\Phi_{0}
is bijective.

Proof.

We construct the inverse of
\Phi_{0}
as follows.

Denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}\subset D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
the sum of the ideals (1) and (3) from Definition
7.2
. By PBW’s theorem, we have

D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\simeq U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}).

The characters

0_{\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}}:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\quad\text{ and }\quad\chi:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

induce
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebra homomorphisms

U_{\hslash}(0_{\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}}):U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\hslash]\quad\text{ and }\quad U_{\hslash}(\chi):U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\hslash]

respectively, and hence splittings

U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\hslash]\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}\cdot U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee})\quad\text{ and }\quad U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\hslash]\oplus\ker U_{\hslash}(\chi).

This yields a splitting

(7.6)

D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\simeq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\oplus\left(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))\right).

Now define

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}^{\prime}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\rightarrow M/M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}

by

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}^{\prime}([z\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}]):=[z\otimes\widetilde{\varphi}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}]\qquad z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}),~\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})).

We show that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}^{\prime}
descends to

\Phi_{0}^{\prime}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}\rightarrow M/M_{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}.

This will complete the proof because
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}
will clearly be the inverse of
\Phi_{0}
. It is clear that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}^{\prime}
sends the subspace (4) from Definition
7.2
to 0. It remains to deal with the left ideal (2). By (
7.6
) and the fact that
e_{\beta^{\vee}_{1}}^{R}-\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}_{1}})
commutes with
e_{\beta^{\vee}_{2}}^{L}-\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}_{2}})
for any
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in-R^{+}
, every element of the left ideal (2) is equal, modulo
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
, to a sum of elements of the form
z\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}))
where
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
,
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
and
\alpha\in-R^{+}
. By Lemma
7.17
, Lemma
A.1
, Lemma
A.2
and Lemma
A.3
, we have

\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}^{\prime}\left([z\cdot_{-\rho^{\vee}}\widetilde{\varphi}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}))]\right)=[m_{\alpha}(z,\widetilde{\varphi}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}})]=0.

∎

Remark 7.25
.

The construction of
\Phi_{0}
is inspired by Teleman’s interpretation of the Rietsch mirror
[
52
, Section 6.6]
.

7.5.
The map
\Phi_{1}

Denote by
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}\subset D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
the sum of the ideals (1), (2) and (3) from Definition
7.2
. Observe that
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is a subquotient of
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
, i.e.
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\simeq N_{1}/N_{2}
for some subspaces
N_{2}\subseteq N_{1}\subseteq D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
. It is not difficult to see that
N_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
.

Definition 7.26
.

Define

\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}

to be the composition of the two canonical homomorphisms

\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}

and

D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}.

Lemma 7.27
.

\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}
descends to a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map

\Phi_{1}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}.

Proof.

Recall from Definition
7.10
that
\mathcal{W}
is generated by elements of the form
x\cdot a
where
x\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
and
a
is either
a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
for some
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
or
a_{\lambda,v}
for some
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
. We have to show
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}(x\cdot a)=0
. Let
\widetilde{x}\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
be a representative of
x
. It is not hard to see that
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}^{\prime\prime}
is naturally a right
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module and the first canonical homomorphism from Definition
7.26
is
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear. This allows us to assume
\widetilde{x}\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
where
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\subset D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
is generated by
z^{L}
with
z\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
(see (
7.6
)). By Lemma
7.9
,
x\cdot a_{\lambda,v}
(resp.
x\cdot(a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]})
) is represented by (resp.
\widetilde{x}(f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]})
plus) a sum of elements of the form
\widetilde{x}zf_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}
where
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee})
and
v^{\prime}\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
. Observe that
f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
and
f_{\lambda,v^{\prime}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\equiv 0
for any
v^{\prime}\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
. It follows that these representatives belong to the sum of the subspaces (3) and (4) from Definition
7.2
, and hence
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}(x\cdot a)=0
as desired.
∎

Lemma 7.28
.

\Phi_{1}
is graded.

Proof.

This is because
\Phi_{1}
is induced by
\id_{D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))}
.
∎

Lemma 7.29
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, we have

\Phi_{1}\circ\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{1}

where
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
come from Lemma
7.6
and Lemma
7.15
respectively.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the definitions of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
,
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
and
\Phi_{1}
.
∎

Proposition 7.30
.

\Phi_{1}
is surjective.

Proof.

Consider the operators
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) from Lemma
7.6
and Lemma
7.15
respectively. They are invertible (Lemma
7.7
and Lemma
7.16
) and satisfy
\Phi_{1}\circ\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{1}
(Lemma
7.29
). Hence it suffices to show that for any
x\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
, there exists
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
y\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}(x)=\Phi_{1}(y)
. Thanks to the ideals (1) and (3) from Definition
7.2
and the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linearity of
\Phi_{1}
, we may assume
x
is represented by some
\varphi\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
.

Let
\lambda_{0}\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
be regular dominant. The vectors
v_{e},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}\in S(\lambda_{0})
give rise to two sections
s_{1}
,
s_{2}
of the line bundle
\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{0}):=G^{\vee}\times^{B_{-}^{\vee}}\mathbb{C}_{\lambda_{0}}
on
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}
respectively. Denote by
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
the zero loci of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
respectively. Define
U:=(G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-})\setminus(D_{1}\cup D_{2})
. Observe that the morphism
G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})\rightarrow G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}\times Z(L^{\vee})
defined by
(g,t)\mapsto(g^{-1}B_{-}^{\vee},t)
takes
X^{\vee}_{P}
isomorphically onto a closed subscheme of
U\times Z(L^{\vee})
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is regular dominant,
\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{0})
is ample, and hence every regular function on
U\times Z(L^{\vee})
is an
\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear combination of
s/s_{1}^{n}s_{2}^{n}
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
and
s\in H^{0}(G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-};\mathcal{L}(2n\lambda_{0}))\simeq S(2n\lambda_{0})
. It is not hard to see that the pull-back of
s/s_{1}^{n}s_{2}^{n}
via the above morphism is of the form
f_{2n\lambda_{0},v}/f_{\lambda_{0},v_{e}}^{n}f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}^{n}
where
v\in S(2n\lambda_{0})
. Therefore, we can find
n,N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
c_{1},\ldots,c_{N}\in\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{N}\in S(2n\lambda_{0})
such that

f_{\lambda_{0},v_{e}}^{n}f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}^{n}\varphi|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}f_{2n\lambda_{0},v_{i}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}.

Notice that
f_{\lambda_{0},v_{e}}^{n}f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}^{n}\varphi
represents
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{n\lambda_{0}}(q^{n[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})]}x)
and each
f_{2n\lambda_{0},v_{i}}
represents an element of
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
, and hence
\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}f_{2n\lambda_{0},v_{i}}
represents
\Phi_{1}(y)
where
y:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[[f_{2n\lambda_{0},v_{i}}]\otimes c_{i}\right]\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
. The proof is complete.
∎

Proposition 7.31
.

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
is bijective

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
7.32
below.
∎

Lemma 7.32
.

There are canonical isomorphisms of
\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-modules

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z}_{P})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})

whose composition is equal to
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
, where

\mathcal{Z}_{P}:=\left\{(b,\xi,t)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\times Z(L^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi,~b\in U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tU^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}.

Here,
e
is defined in (
4.2
) and the
\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure on
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z}_{P})
is defined as in Lemma
6.19
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
is right exact,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})))\simeq\mathcal{O}(T^{*}G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
by a subspace which is the sum of the subspaces obtained by applying
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
to (1) to (4) from Definition
7.2
. It is not difficult to see that this subspace is in fact an ideal so that
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})
is a ring, and

\displaystyle\spec\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})

(7.7)

\displaystyle\simeq~

\displaystyle\left\{(g,\xi,t)\in G^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})\left|~\xi\in e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee},~g\cdot\xi\in e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-},~(g,t)\in X^{\vee}_{P}\right.\right\}

as schemes. Here, as in the proof of Lemma
6.19
, we have identified
T^{*}G^{\vee}
, first with
G^{\vee}\times(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{*}
via left translations, and then with
G^{\vee}\times\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
via
\beta
. By Kostant’s slice theorem, we have

b\cdot\xi\in e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-}\Longleftrightarrow b\cdot\xi=\xi

for any
b\in B^{\vee}
and
\xi\in e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
. Therefore, the RHS of (
7.5
) is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Z}_{P}
.

Again, by the right exactness of
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W})
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})))
by the ideal generated by
a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) and
a_{\lambda,v}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
,
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
). (More precisely, the elements of
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})))
they represent.) By Lemma
7.9
and an induction argument, this ideal is equal to the ideal generated by
f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) and
f_{\lambda,v}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
,
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
). By Lemma
6.19
,

(7.8)

\spec\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K}))\simeq\mathcal{Z}=\left\{(b,\xi)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi\right.\right\}.

Notice that
Z(L^{\vee})
is a subtorus of
T^{\vee}
and
q^{[w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)]}=q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}\in\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
. It follows that, by (
7.8
) and Lemma
A.4
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W})

\displaystyle\simeq~

\displaystyle\left\{(b,\xi,t)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\times Z(L^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi,~b\in U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tU^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}=\mathcal{Z}_{P}.

This gives us the desired isomorphisms. The last assertion that their composition is equal to
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
follows from the observation that all the maps involved are induced by the identity map of
\mathcal{O}(T^{*}G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
.
∎

7.6.
The map
\Phi_{2}

Recall the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear maps

\Phi_{GMP}^{T}:H^{\widehat{T}}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)\rightarrow QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

and

\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\rightarrow H^{\widehat{T}}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)

defined in Definition
5.11
and Definition
6.16
respectively.

Definition 7.33
.

(1)

Define

\Psi_{GMP}^{T}:=(\Phi_{GMP}^{T})_{\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]}:H^{\widehat{T}}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]\rightarrow QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

to be the map obtained from
\Phi_{GMP}^{T}
by extension of scalars and

\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}:=\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}\otimes\mirror:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow H^{\widehat{T}}_{-\bullet}(\mathcal{G}r)[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

where
\mirror:\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
is defined in (
2.1
).

(2)

Define
\Psi_{GMP}^{G}
and
\Psi_{BF}^{\kappa}
similarly.

Definition 7.34
.

Define a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map

\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}:=\Psi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}].

Lemma 7.35
.

\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}
descends to a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map

\Phi_{2}:\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}\rightarrow QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}].

Proof.

Let
x\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
and
a\in\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
. Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is a homomorphism of modules with respect to the ring isomorphism
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
(Theorem
6.15
) and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{G}
is a module action (Proposition
5.9
), we have

\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}(x\cdot a)=\Psi_{GMP}^{T}\left(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(x)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}\Psi_{BF}^{\kappa}(a)\right)=\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(x)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}\left(\Psi_{GMP}^{G}(\Psi_{BF}^{\kappa}(a))\right).

Therefore, it suffices to show

(7.9)

\Psi_{GMP}^{G}(\Psi_{BF}^{\kappa}(a))=0

if
a=a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
with
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
or
a=a_{\lambda,v}
with

\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
. (See Definition
7.10
.)

By Proposition
6.17
,
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}])
is equal to
[Z]
where
Z
is the unique MV cycle of type
\lambda
and weight
w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)
. Since
[Z]
has degree
2\rho(\lambda-w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda))
, it follows that, by Proposition
5.15
,
\Phi_{GMP}^{T}([Z])=cq^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}
for some
c\in\mathbb{C}
. To determine
c
, it suffices to look at
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{GMP}^{T})
which is Peterson-Lam-Shimozono’s homomorphism
\Phi_{PLS}
(see Proposition
5.12
). Hence, by
[
10
, Lemma 6.3]
, we have
c=1
. It follows that

(7.10)

\Psi_{GMP}^{T}(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}]-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}))=0.

On the other hand, for any
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
, we have
