\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v}])\in H^{\widehat{T}}_{>2\rho(\lambda-w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda))}(\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda})
, and hence, by Proposition
5.14
, we have

(7.11)

\Psi_{GMP}^{T}(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{\lambda,v}]))=0.

Now, (
7.9
) follows from (
7.10
), (
7.11
) and the fact that
\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}
and
\Psi_{GMP}^{T}
are induced by
\Psi_{BF}^{\kappa}
and
\Psi_{GMP}^{G}
respectively. (Recall from Definition
7.8
that
a_{\lambda,v}
is characterized by the equality
[f_{\lambda,v}]=a_{\lambda,v}+\sum_{i=2}^{|W|}z_{i}\cdot\kappa_{\hslash}(f_{\lambda})(\widetilde{a}_{\lambda,v,i})
.)
∎

Lemma 7.36
.

\Phi_{2}
is graded.

Proof.

This is because
\Phi_{GMP}^{T}
and
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
are graded (Lemma
5.6
and Theorem
6.15
(2)).
∎

Lemma 7.37
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, we have

\Phi_{2}\circ\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{2}

where
\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}
come from Lemma
3.4
and Lemma
7.15
respectively.

Proof.

It suffices to show
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}\circ\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}\circ\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}.
From the second paragraph of the proof of Lemma
7.16
, we have

\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}\circ\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\lambda}=\left(([t^{\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]}\right)\circ\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}.

Hence it suffices to show

(7.12)

\Phi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\left([t^{\lambda}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}-\right)=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{GMP}^{T}.

Since both sides of (
7.12
) are
(-\lambda)
-twisted
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)
-linear, we may assume the given input element belongs to
H_{-\bullet}^{\widehat{G}}(\mathcal{G}r)
. In this case, we apply Proposition
5.9
to get the result.
∎

Proposition 7.38
.

\Phi_{2}
is surjective.

Proof.

This follows from the facts that
\Psi_{GMP}^{T}
is surjective (Corollary
5.13
) and
\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}
is surjective (Theorem
6.15
).
∎

Proposition 7.39
.

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})
is bijective.

Proof.

Recall the following

(1)

\mathcal{Z}:=\{(b,\xi)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})|~b\cdot\xi=\xi\}
(see Lemma
6.19
);

(2)

\mathcal{Z}_{P}:=\left\{(b,\xi,t)\in B^{\vee}\times(e+\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\times Z(L^{\vee})\left|~b\cdot\xi=\xi,~b\in U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tU^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}
(see Lemma
7.32
);

(3)

B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}:=\{(b,h)\in B^{\vee}\times\spec H_{T}^{\bullet}(\pt)|~b\cdot e^{T}(h)=e^{T}(h)\}
(see (
6.5
));

(4)

\Phi_{YZ}:\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{-\bullet}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r)
, Yun-Zhu’s isomorphism
[
53
]
(see Proposition
6.20
); and

(5)

\Phi_{PLS}:H_{-\bullet}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r)\rightarrow QH_{T}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
, Peterson-Lam-Shimozono’s homomorphism
[
9
,
32
,
43
]
(see Proposition
5.12
).

For simplicity, put

N_{1}:=H_{-\bullet}^{T}(\mathcal{G}r)[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]\quad\text{ and }\quad N_{2}:=QH_{T}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}].

We have the following commutative diagram

(7.13)

Here,

(i)

the leftmost commutative square is induced by the commutative diagram

\mathcal{Z}_{P}
\mathcal{Z}\times Z(L^{\vee})
B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}\times_{G^{\vee}}U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}B_{-}^{\vee}
B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee})
canonical
induced by right arrow
\simeq
(b,h,t)\mapsto(b,e^{T}(h),t)
\simeq
(b,h)\mapsto(b,h,t(b))

where
t(b)\in T^{\vee}
is the unique element such that

(7.14)

b\in U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}t(b)U^{\vee}_{-}

(by the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 6.10]
,
t(b)
in fact lies in
Z(L^{\vee})
);

(ii)

the middle commutative square is given by Lemma
6.19
and Proposition
6.20
; and

(iii)

the rightmost commutative square is given by Proposition
5.12
.

By Lemma
7.32
, we have

(7.15)

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})\circ\eta_{1}=\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Psi_{GMP}^{T})\circ\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}).

We wish to show

(7.16)

\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{4}=(\Phi_{PLS})_{\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]}\circ(\Phi_{YZ}\otimes\mirror)

where
\Phi_{loc}
is the algebra isomorphism from
[
10
, Theorem B]
. This will complete the proof of Proposition
7.39
because we will have
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})=\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{2}
by (
7.13
) and (
7.15
). By the definition of
\Phi_{loc}
(see
[
10
, Definition 6.7]
), we have
\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{4}|_{\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})}=\Phi_{PLS}\circ\Phi_{YZ}
. Hence it remains to show that the restrictions to
\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
of both sides of (
7.16
) are equal. Let
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
be anti-dominant. By definition,
\eta_{4}
sends
\lambda|_{Z(L^{\vee})}\in\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
to the regular function
(b,h)\mapsto\lambda(t(b))
. Consider the regular function
g_{P,w_{0}(\lambda)}\in\mathcal{O}(B_{e^{T}}^{\vee})
defined by
(b,h)\mapsto\langle v^{*}_{w_{0}(\lambda)},b\cdot v_{w_{P}w_{0}}\rangle
. By (
7.14
) and
[
10
, Proposition B.7]
,
\eta_{4}(g_{P,w_{0}(\lambda)})
is the regular function
(b,h)\mapsto w_{P}w_{0}(w_{0}(\lambda))(t(b))=w_{P}(\lambda)(t(b))
. Since
t(b)\in Z(L^{\vee})
, we have
w_{P}(\lambda)(t(b))=\lambda(t(b))
, and hence
\eta_{4}(g_{P,w_{0}(\lambda)})=\eta_{4}(\lambda|_{Z(L^{\vee})})
. By
[
10
, Lemma 6.3]
,
\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{4}(g_{P,w_{0}(\lambda)})=q^{[\lambda]}
. Therefore,

\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{4}(\lambda|_{Z(L^{\vee})})=\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{4}(g_{P,w_{0}(\lambda)})=q^{[\lambda]}=(\Phi_{PLS})_{\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]}\circ(\Phi_{YZ}\otimes\mirror)(\lambda|_{Z(L^{\vee})}).

Since
\lambda|_{Z(L^{\vee})}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
anti-dominant) and their inverses generate the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
, the result follows.
∎

Remark 7.40
.

From the proof of Proposition
7.39
(more precisely, the equality
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})=\Phi_{loc}\circ\eta_{2}
), we see that
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})
is a ring map where the ring structure on
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W})
is induced by
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{Z}_{P})
via Lemma
7.32
. We will use this fact when we prove that
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{mir})
is a ring map in Section
7.9
.

7.7.
The map
\Phi_{3}

Proposition 7.41
.

There exists a unique
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map
\Phi_{3}
such that
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{1}
is surjective (Proposition
7.30
), the existence of
\Phi_{3}
is equivalent to
\ker\Phi_{1}\subseteq\ker\Phi_{2}
. Let
y\in\ker\Phi_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
is bijective (Proposition
7.31
), we have
y=\hslash y_{1}
for some
y_{1}\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
. Then
0=\Phi_{1}(y)=\hslash\Phi_{1}(y_{1})
. By Proposition
B.2
and Proposition
7.24
,
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
is
\hslash
-torsion-free. Hence we have
y_{1}\in\ker\Phi_{1}
. Continuing, we obtain
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\ldots\in\ker\Phi_{1}
satisfying
y=\hslash^{k}y_{k}
for any
k
. It follows that
\Phi_{2}(y)=\hslash^{k}\Phi_{2}(y_{k})
for any
k
, and hence
\Phi_{2}(y)=0
because
QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
is
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-free. This establishes the existence of
\Phi_{3}
. The uniqueness follows from the surjectivity of
\Phi_{1}
.
∎

Lemma 7.42
.

\Phi_{3}
is graded.

Proof.

This is because
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
are graded (Lemma
7.28
and Lemma
7.36
),
\Phi_{1}
is surjective (Proposition
7.30
) and
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
).
∎

Lemma 7.43
.

For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
, we have

\Phi_{3}\circ\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{3}

where
\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\mathcal{V}}_{\lambda}
come from Lemma
3.4
and Lemma
7.6
respectively.

Proof.

This follows from the analogous equalities for
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
(Lemma
7.29
and Lemma
7.37
), the surjectivity of
\Phi_{1}
(Proposition
7.30
) and the equality
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
).
∎

Proposition 7.44
.

\Phi_{3}
is bijective.

Proof.

The surjectivity of
\Phi_{3}
follows from that of
\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.38
) and the equality
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
). It remains to show that
\Phi_{3}
is injective.

Since
\Phi_{3}
is surjective and
QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
is a free
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
-module, we have a (non-canonical) splitting

D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}\simeq\ker\Phi_{3}\oplus QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}].

By Proposition
B.4
and Proposition
7.24
,
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee})}
-module, and hence
\ker\Phi_{3}
is also coherent. It follows that
\ker\Phi_{3}=0
, i.e.
\Phi_{3}
is injective, if we can show that the fiber dimension of
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
at every point
(\hslash,h,t)\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee})
is at most
|W/W_{P}|
, the rank of
QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
.

Since
\Phi_{0}
is bijective (Proposition
7.24
), it suffices to look at
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
. Denote the fiber of
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
at
(\hslash,h,t)
by
G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)
. By Proposition
B.1
, we have
\dim G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)=\dim G_{0}(c\hslash,ch,t)
for any
(\hslash,h,t)\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee})
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
with
c,\hslash\neq 0
. This equality together with the coherence of
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\simeq D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
implies
\dim G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)\leqslant\dim G_{0}(0,0,t)
. But since
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
and
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})
are bijective (Proposition
7.31
and Proposition
7.39
), we have

(7.17)

\dim G_{0}(0,0,t)=\dim G_{0}(0,h,t)=|W/W_{P}|,

and hence we have
\dim G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)\leqslant|W/W_{P}|
for any
(\hslash,h,t)
with
\hslash\neq 0
. It remains to deal with the case
\hslash=0
. But this also follows from (
7.17
).
∎

7.8.
The map
\Phi_{mir}

Definition 7.45
.

Define

\Phi_{mir}:=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{0}:G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\rightarrow QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

where
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{3}
come from Lemma
7.21
and Proposition
7.41
respectively.

Proposition 7.46
.

\Phi_{mir}
is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear.

We will prove Proposition
7.46
after some preparation. Take
\lambda_{0}\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
such that
\alpha_{i}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))
is non-zero precisely when
i\in I\setminus I_{P}
. Let
i\in I\setminus I_{P}
. There is a unique MV cycle of type
\lambda_{0}
and weight
w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}
. In fact, by
[
10
, Lemma A.1]
, this MV cycle is equal to
\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{s_{\alpha_{i}}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))}}
. Let
v_{i}\in S(\lambda_{0})_{w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}}
correspond to this MV cycle via the composite isomorphism (
6.2
). Define
f_{i}:=f_{\lambda_{0},v_{i}}
. Define also
f_{0}:=f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}=f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{0}}}
. (For the definition of
f_{\lambda,v}
, see the paragraph before Proposition
6.17
. For the definition of
v_{w}
, see the paragraph before Definition
7.10
.)

Define

\widetilde{A}_{i}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))

by

\widetilde{A}_{i}(xq^{[\mu]}):=x(f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0})q^{[\mu-w_{0}(\lambda_{0})-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+\hslash x\partial_{q_{i}}q^{[\mu]}

for any
x\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}
and
q^{[\mu]}\in\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
, where
\partial_{q_{i}}q^{[\mu]}:=\omega_{i}(\mu)q^{[\mu-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}
.

Lemma 7.47
.

\widetilde{A}_{i}
preserves
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\widetilde{A}_{i}
preserves the ideal (3) from Definition
7.2
. By Lemma
A.5
, both
f_{i}-\omega_{i}^{L}f_{0}
and
f_{0}
represent elements of
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
, and so does
f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}
. It follows that
\widetilde{A}_{i}
maps the ideals (1) and (2) into
\mathcal{V}
. It remains to handle the subspace (4). Let
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
such that
\widetilde{\varphi}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\equiv 0
. We have

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{i}(z\widetilde{\varphi})=~

\displaystyle z\widetilde{\varphi}(f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0})q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+\hslash z\partial_{q_{i}}\widetilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle z\widetilde{\varphi}f_{i}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}-\omega_{i}^{L}z\widetilde{\varphi}f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i})z\widetilde{\varphi}f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}

\displaystyle+\hslash z(\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i}^{L}}\widetilde{\varphi})f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+\hslash z\partial_{q_{i}}\widetilde{\varphi}.

Clearly the first three terms in the last expression lie in
\mathcal{V}
. Moreover, we have

(7.18)

f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\equiv q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}.

(We apply the equality
(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}\cdot v_{w_{P}w_{0}}=v_{e}
which is
[
10
, Proposition B.7]
.) Therefore,

\widetilde{A}_{i}(z\widetilde{\varphi})\equiv\hslash z\mathcal{L}_{q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}+\partial_{q_{i}}}\widetilde{\varphi}~(\bmod{\mathcal{V}}).

The result now follows from Lemma
A.6
which says that the vector field
\widetilde{\eta}_{i}:=q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}+\partial_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
is tangent to
X^{\vee}_{P}
.
∎

Definition 7.48
.

Define

A_{i}:D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}\rightarrow D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}

to be the map induced by
\widetilde{A}_{i}
via Lemma
7.47
.

Recall
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
is the
\mathbb{C}[\hslash]
-algebra generated by
q_{i}^{\pm 1}
and
\xi_{i}
(
i\in I\setminus I_{P}
) subject to the relations
q_{i}q_{j}=q_{j}q_{i}
,
\xi_{i}q_{j}-q_{j}\xi_{i}=\hslash\delta_{ij}
and
\xi_{i}\xi_{j}=\xi_{j}\xi_{i}
. Since
\Phi_{0}
is bijective (Proposition
7.24
), there is a unique
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-module structure on
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
such that
\Phi_{0}
is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear.

Lemma 7.49
.

For any
i\in I\setminus I_{P}
, the operator
\xi_{i}\cdot-\in\eendo(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})
coincides with the operator
A_{i}
from Definition
7.48
.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{\eta}_{i}:=q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}+\partial_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
. By Lemma
A.6
,
\widetilde{\eta}_{i}
is tangent to
X^{\vee}_{P}
and
\eta_{i}:=\widetilde{\eta}_{i}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
is a lift of
\partial_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}(Z(L^{\vee}))
with respect to
\pi
. Every element of
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
is represented by
z\widetilde{\varphi}
for some
z\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
. Put
\varphi:=\widetilde{\varphi}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
. By the definitions of the
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-module structures on
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
and
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
, we have

\displaystyle\xi_{i}\cdot[z\widetilde{\varphi}]=~

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}\left(\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}^{-1}(z)\otimes\left(\hslash\left(\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}\right)\varphi+\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}\varphi+(\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}W)\varphi\right){\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\sum_{j}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\sum_{j}\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}^{-1}(z)h_{j}\otimes(\iota_{\eta_{i}}p^{*}\langle h^{j},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle)\varphi\right).

(See Definition
7.20
and Definition
4.11
for the definitions of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{0}
and
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
respectively.) By Lemma
A.7
,
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=0
. By Lemma
A.8
,
q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}f_{i}
is an extension of
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}W
. By Lemma
A.9
,
\iota_{\eta_{i}}p^{*}\langle h^{j},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle=q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\langle h^{j},\omega_{i}\rangle
. Therefore,

\displaystyle~\xi_{i}\cdot[z\widetilde{\varphi}]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left[z\left(\hslash\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{\eta}_{i}}\widetilde{\varphi}+q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}f_{i}\widetilde{\varphi}\right)-z(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\widetilde{\varphi}\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\left[\hslash z\mathcal{L}_{q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}+\partial_{q_{i}}}\widetilde{\varphi}+z\widetilde{\varphi}(f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0})q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.z(\omega_{i}^{L}\widetilde{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\omega_{i}^{L})q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+z\widetilde{\varphi}(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))\left(f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}-q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\right)\right].

By (
7.18
), the term
z\widetilde{\varphi}(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))\left(f_{0}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}-q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\right)
belongs to the subspace (4) from Definition
7.2
. Moreover, we have
z(\omega_{i}^{L}\widetilde{\varphi}-\widetilde{\varphi}\omega_{i}^{L})q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}=\hslash z\mathcal{L}_{q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}}\widetilde{\varphi}
. Therefore,

\xi_{i}\cdot[z\widetilde{\varphi}]=\left[z\widetilde{\varphi}(f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0})q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}+\hslash z\partial_{q_{i}}\widetilde{\varphi}\right]=\left[\widetilde{A}_{i}(z\widetilde{\varphi})\right]=A_{i}([z\widetilde{\varphi}]).

∎

Proof of Proposition
7.46
.

By definition,
\Phi_{0}
is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear. Hence it suffices to prove that
\Phi_{3}
is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear. Let
x\in D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
. Since
\Phi_{1}
is surjective (Proposition
7.30
), there exists
y\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
such that
\Phi_{1}(y)=x
. Let
\widetilde{y}\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
be a representative of
y
. Let
i\in I\setminus I_{P}
. Denote by
[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}]\in\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
the element represented by
f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}
. (See Lemma
A.5
.) We have

A_{i}(x)=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}\left(\widetilde{y}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\cdot[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}]+\hslash\partial_{q_{i}}\widetilde{y}\right).

By Lemma
7.49
, the equality
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
) and the definition of
\Phi_{2}
(Lemma
7.35
), we have

(7.19)

\Phi_{3}(\xi_{i}\cdot x)=\Phi_{3}(A_{i}(x))=\Psi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}\left(\widetilde{y}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\cdot[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}]+\hslash\partial_{q_{i}}\widetilde{y}\right).

Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is a homomorphism of modules with respect to the ring isomorphism
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
(Theorem
6.15
) and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{G}
is a module action (Proposition
5.9
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}\left(\widetilde{y}q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\cdot[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}]\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\Psi_{GMP}^{T}\left(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(\widetilde{y})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}r}^{T}\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}\left(\left[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}\right]\right)\right)

(7.20)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(\widetilde{y})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}\left(\Phi_{GMP}^{G}\circ\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}\left(\left[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}\right]\right)\right).

By Proposition
6.17
and
[
10
, Lemma A.1]
, we have

\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{i}])=\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{s_{\alpha_{i}}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))}}\right]\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}([f_{0}])=\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{w_{0}(\lambda_{0})}}\right].

(By our assumption on
\lambda_{0}
, we have
w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda_{0})=w_{0}(\lambda_{0})
.) Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is an extension of
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
and is
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear where the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module structure on
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is given by the left multiplication of left-invariant vector fields twisted by
\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}
(see Example
6.4
and Definition
6.7
), it follows that

\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}\left(\left[f_{i}-(\omega_{i}^{L}-\hslash\rho^{\vee}(\omega_{i}))f_{0}\right]\right)=\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{s_{\alpha_{i}}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))}}\right]-\omega_{i}\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{w_{0}(\lambda_{0})}}\right].

Now by Proposition
5.12
,

(7.21)

\Phi_{GMP}^{G}\left(\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{s_{\alpha_{i}}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))}}\right]-\omega_{i}\left[\overline{\mathcal{B}\cdot t^{w_{0}(\lambda_{0})}}\right]\right)=q^{[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})]}(\sigma_{s_{\alpha_{i}}}-\omega_{i})=q^{[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})]}c_{1}^{\widehat{G}}(L_{\omega_{i}}).

Combining (
7.19
), (
7.8
) and (
7.21
) gives

(7.22)

\Phi_{3}(\xi_{i}\cdot x)=q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(\widetilde{y})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}\left(q^{[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})]}c_{1}^{\widehat{G}}(L_{\omega_{i}})\right)+\hslash\partial_{q_{i}}\left(\Psi_{BF}^{\delta}(\widetilde{y})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}1\right).

Write
\widetilde{y}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{y}_{j}\otimes q^{[\mu_{j}]}
with
\widetilde{y}_{j}\in\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
. Put
\widetilde{Y}_{j}:=\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}(\widetilde{y}_{j})
. The RHS of (
7.22
) is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\widetilde{Y}_{j}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}\left(\left(q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}c_{1}^{\widehat{G}}(L_{\omega_{i}})+\hslash\partial_{q_{i}}\right)q^{[\mu_{j}]}\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\sum_{j}\widetilde{Y}_{j}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}\left(\nabla^{G}_{\partial_{q_{i}}}q^{[\mu_{j}]}\right)=\sum_{j}\nabla^{T}_{\partial_{q_{i}}}\left(\widetilde{Y}_{j}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{T}q^{[\mu_{j}]}\right)=\nabla^{T}_{\partial_{q_{i}}}\left(\Phi_{3}(x)\right)

where the second equality follows from Proposition
5.10
. The proof is complete.

\square

7.9.
Conclusion of proof

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Define
\Phi_{mir}
to be the map from Definition
7.45
.

(1)

\Phi_{mir}
is bijective.
This is because
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{3}
are bijective (Proposition
7.24
and Proposition
7.44
).

(2)

\Phi_{mir}
is
D_{\hslash,Z(L^{\vee})}
-linear.
This is Proposition
7.46
.

(3)

\Phi_{mir}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])=1
.
Consider the elements of
D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V}
and
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
represented by
1
. By abuse of notation, we denote both of them by
[1]
. By the definition of
\Phi_{0}
, we have
\Phi_{0}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])=[1]
. It is clear that
\Phi_{1}([1])=[1]
. By Proposition
6.17
(applied to
f_{0,v_{e}}=1
) and Proposition
5.12
, we have
\Phi_{2}([1])=1
. Since
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
), we have

\Phi_{mir}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{0}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])=\Phi_{3}([1])=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}([1])=\Phi_{2}([1])=1.

(4)

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{mir})
is a ring isomorphism.
Identify
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p))
with
\jac(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
via Lemma
4.10
where the fiberwise volume form is taken to be
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
from Definition
4.11
. By Lemma
7.32
, both
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(D_{\hslash,G^{\vee}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{V})
and
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W})
are naturally rings. It is clear that
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{0})
and
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
are ring maps. By Remark
7.40
,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{2})
is also a ring map. Since
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
) and
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{1})
is bijective (Proposition
7.31
),
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{3})
and hence
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{mir})
is a ring map.

(5)

\Phi_{mir}\circ\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}\circ\Phi_{mir}
for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
.
By
\mathbb{S}^{A/B}_{\lambda_{1}}\circ\mathbb{S}^{A/B}_{\lambda_{2}}=\mathbb{S}^{A/B}_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}
(Lemma
3.6
and Lemma
4.18
), we may assume
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
. The result follows from the analogous results for
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{3}
(Lemma
7.23
and Lemma
7.43
).

(6)

\Phi_{mir}
is graded.
This is because
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{3}
are graded (Lemma
7.22
and Lemma
7.42
).

Finally, we prove that any
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear map satisfying (3) and (5) must be equal to
\Phi_{mir}
. Let
\Phi^{\prime}
be such a map. For any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
, we have

\Phi^{\prime}(\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}]))=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}(\Phi^{\prime}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}]))=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}(1)=\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}(\Phi_{mir}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}]))=\Phi_{mir}(\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])).

The result will be proved if we can show that
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
is additively generated by
\mathbb{S}^{B}_{\lambda}([\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}])
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
) over
\fof(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}))\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
. Since
\Phi_{mir}
satisfies (1), (3) and (5), it suffices to show that
QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
is additively generated by
\mathbb{S}^{A}_{\lambda}(1)
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
) over
\fof(H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt))\otimes\mathbb{C}[q_{i}^{\pm 1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]
. This follows from Corollary
5.13
and the fact that
H_{\bullet}^{\widehat{T}}(\mathcal{G}r)
is additively generated by
[t^{\lambda}]
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}
) over
\fof(H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt))
.

\square

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Notice that

\widetilde{\Phi}_{R}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{0}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}|_{\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})},\quad\Phi_{YZ}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi_{PLS}^{\hslash}=\Phi_{GMP}^{T}.

The commutativity of the diagram follows from
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}|_{\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})}=\Phi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
(Definition
7.34
),
\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}
(Proposition
7.41
) and
\Phi_{mir}=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{0}
(Definition
7.45
). The uniqueness follows from the surjectivity of
\Phi_{1}
(Proposition
7.30
).

\square

Appendix A
Proofs from preceding sections

We prove several lemmas which are used in the preceding sections.

Lemma A.1
.

(Used in the proof of Lemma
7.21
) For any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
,
\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})
is an extension of
\frac{\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}{\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}}
.

Proof.

Define
\mathcal{U}:=U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}P_{-}^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}
, the open Schubert cell in
G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}
. Notice that
\mathcal{U}_{P}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
where
\mathcal{U}_{P}
is defined in Section
4.1
(before Lemma
4.5
). Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{U})
be a defining function of the reduced closed subscheme
\mathcal{U}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{P}
. By the assumption on
\omega_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}
(see the paragraph before Definition
4.11
),
f|_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\omega_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}
extends to a volume form
\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
on
\mathcal{U}
. Define
\overline{\omega}_{\mathcal{U}}:=(\pr_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\circ\nu)^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
and
\overline{f}:=f\circ\pr_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\circ\nu
so that
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=\overline{f}^{-1}\overline{\omega}_{\mathcal{U}}
. (Recall
\nu
comes from Lemma
4.5
.) Lemma
A.1
will be proved if we can show

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\overline{\omega}_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle=0

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\overline{f}

\displaystyle=-\overline{f}\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}.

Let us prove (
A.1
) first. Let
V_{\alpha^{\vee}}\in\mathfrak{X}(G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee})
be the vector field generated by the
\mathbb{G}_{a}
-action
s\mapsto\psi_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{s}:=\exp(se_{\alpha^{\vee}})\cdot-
. Observe that
e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}
is a lift of
V_{\alpha^{\vee}}
with respect to the projection
\pi_{G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}}:G^{\vee}\rightarrow G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}
. Since the left translates of
\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
are tangent to the fibers of
\pi_{G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}}
, it follows that
\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})
is also a lift of
V_{\alpha^{\vee}}
with respect to
\pi_{G^{\vee}/P_{-}^{\vee}}
, and hence
\nu_{*}\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}=V_{\alpha^{\vee}}|_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\oplus 0
. Thus, (
A.1
) is equivalent to
\mathcal{L}_{V_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}=0
. Observe that for each
s
,
(\psi_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{s})^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
is a volume form on
\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is an affine space, there is a morphism
\mathbb{G}_{a}\ni s\mapsto c_{s}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that
(\psi_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{s})^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}=c_{s}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}
. Then
c_{s}
is necessarily constant (in fact
\equiv 1
). This gives

\mathcal{L}_{V_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}=\left.\frac{d}{ds}(\psi_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{s})^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{U}}\right|_{s=0}=0

as desired.

It remains to prove (
A.2
). We first determine
f
as follows. Identify
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}
with
G^{\vee}_{sc}/B^{\vee}_{sc,-}
where
G^{\vee}_{sc}
is the universal covering of
G^{\vee}
and
B^{\vee}_{sc,-}
is the Borel subgroup of
G^{\vee}_{sc}
lying over
B_{-}^{\vee}
. Let
\{\omega^{\vee}_{1},\ldots,\omega^{\vee}_{r}\}
be the dual basis of
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\}
. For any
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
, define
\mathcal{L}(\omega^{\vee}_{i}):=G^{\vee}_{sc}\times^{B^{\vee}_{sc,-}}\mathbb{C}_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}
and
S(\omega^{\vee}_{i}):=H^{0}(G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-};\mathcal{L}(\omega^{\vee}_{i}))
. Then
\mathcal{L}(\omega^{\vee}_{i})
is a line bundle on
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}
and
S(\omega^{\vee}_{i})
is a representation of
G^{\vee}_{sc}
(in fact the
i
-th fundamental representation). Take a non-zero vector
v_{e}\in S(\omega^{\vee}_{i})_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}
(i.e. highest weight vector). Let
v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*}\in S(\omega^{\vee}_{i})^{*}
be the unique vector satisfying
v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*}|_{S(\omega^{\vee}_{i})_{\neq\omega^{\vee}_{i}}}=0
and
\langle v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*},v_{e}\rangle=1
. Define
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}_{sc})
by
\varphi_{i}(g):=\langle v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*},g^{-1}\cdot v_{e}\rangle
. Then
\varphi_{i}
descends to a section of
\mathcal{L}(\omega^{\vee}_{i})
whose scheme-theoretic zero locus is equal to
\overline{U^{\vee}\dot{s}_{\alpha_{i}}B_{-}^{\vee}/B_{-}^{\vee}}
. Define
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}:=\exp\left(\bigoplus_{\beta\in w_{0}(R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P})}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\beta^{\vee}}\right)\subseteq U^{\vee}_{-}
. Notice that the morphism
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
defined by
u\mapsto u(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}P_{-}^{\vee}
is an isomorphism. By the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{P}
and the fact that every
B^{\vee}_{-}
-orbit in
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}
intersects every
B^{\vee}
-orbit transversely, we have, after possibly rescaling
f
,

(A.3)

f(u(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}P_{-}^{\vee})=(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{r})(u(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1})

for any
u\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. (The points
u
,
\dot{w}_{P}
and
\dot{w}_{0}
have natural lifts in
G^{\vee}_{sc}
. By abuse of notation, we denote these lifts by the same symbols.)

Next, we express
\overline{f}
in terms of some known functions on
X^{\vee}_{P}
based on what we have obtained from the previous paragraph. Let
x=(g,t)\in X^{\vee}_{P}
. There are
u_{0}\in U^{\vee}
,
t_{0}\in T^{\vee}
and
u_{1},u_{2}\in U^{\vee}_{-}
such that

g=u_{0}t_{0}=u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tu_{2}.

These points are unique if we require
u_{1}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Put
u_{2}^{\prime}:=tu_{2}t^{-1}\in U^{\vee}_{-}
. We have

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}(u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1})

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*},(u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1})^{-1}\cdot v_{e}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*},(u_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1})^{-1}u_{0}\cdot v_{e}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle v_{\omega^{\vee}_{i}}^{*},tt_{0}^{-1}\cdot v_{e}\rangle

\displaystyle=\omega^{\vee}_{i}(tt_{0}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\omega^{\vee}_{i}\circ(\pi/p))(x).

(Recall we are abusing notation. The point
tt_{0}^{-1}
is actually a lift of the corresponding point in
T^{\vee}
. It depends on the natural lifts of
u_{0}
,
u_{1}
,
u_{2}^{\prime}
,
\dot{w}_{P}
and
\dot{w}_{0}
.) Therefore, by (
A.3
),

\overline{f}(x)=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\varphi_{i}\right)(u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\omega^{\vee}_{i}\circ(\pi/p))(x)=(\rho^{\vee}\circ(\pi/p))(x).

Finally, we show
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}(\rho^{\vee}\circ(\pi/p))=-(\rho^{\vee}\circ(\pi/p))\rho^{\vee}(\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}})|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
. This will give (
A.2
). It suffices to show
\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}(\pi/p)^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}=-\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
. Clearly, we have
\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}\pi^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}=0
. Thus we are done if we can show
\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}p^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}=\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
. This follows from Lemma
A.3
below.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

(Lemma
7.21
) For any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
,
\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}})
is an extension of
\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}W
.

Proof.

By definition,
\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}=(\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})\oplus 0)|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
, and by (
7.3
) we have

\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})=e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}-\sum_{\beta\in-R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}.

Observe that both
e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}
and
e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}
are tangent to
U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}Z(L^{\vee})U^{\vee}_{-}
, and
W
is the restriction of the regular function
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}Z(L^{\vee})U^{\vee}_{-}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
defined by

W^{\prime}(x):=e^{\chi}(u_{1})+e^{\chi}(u_{2})\quad\text{for any }~x=(u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}t_{1}u_{2},t).

It is not difficult to see that

\mathcal{L}_{(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R}\oplus 0)}W^{\prime}=\chi(e_{\alpha^{\vee}})\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{L}_{(e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\oplus 0)}W^{\prime}=\chi(e_{\beta^{\vee}}).

The rest is clear.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

(Lemma
7.21
, Lemma
A.1
) For any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
,
\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}
is an extension of
\iota_{\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}}p^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\zeta_{\alpha^{\vee}}=(\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})\oplus 0)|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
, and by (
7.3
) we have

\pr_{\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}}^{L}(e_{\alpha^{\vee}}^{R})=\sum_{i}\langle h^{i},\theta_{\alpha,\mathfrak{t}}\rangle h_{i}^{L}+\sum_{\beta\in R^{+}}\theta_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}.

Observe that both
h_{i}^{L}
and
e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}
are tangent to
B^{\vee}
, and
p
is the restriction of the regular function
p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(B^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))
defined by

p^{\prime}(x):=t_{0}\quad\text{for any }~x=(u_{0}t_{0},t).

It is not hard to see that

\mathcal{L}_{(h_{i}^{L}\oplus 0)}(p^{\prime})^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}=h_{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{L}_{(e_{\beta^{\vee}}^{L}\oplus 0)}(p^{\prime})^{*}\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}=0.

The rest is clear.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

(Lemma
7.32
) The closed subscheme of
G^{\vee}\times T^{\vee}
defined by
f_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
) and
f_{\lambda,v}
(
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
,
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
) is equal to

\mathfrak{X}_{P}:=\left\{(g,t)\in G^{\vee}\times T^{\vee}\left|~g\in U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tU^{\vee}_{-}\right.\right\}.

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that these equations vanish on
\mathfrak{X}_{P}
. Let
\varphi=\sum_{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}}\varphi_{\lambda}q^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee}\times T^{\vee})
with
\varphi_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
. Suppose
\varphi|_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}=0
. Let
S\subseteq Q^{\vee}
be the set of
\lambda
for which
\varphi_{\lambda}\neq 0
. Then
S
is finite so there exists
\mu_{0}\in Q^{\vee}
such that
\lambda+\mu_{0}\in w_{P}w_{0}Q^{\vee}_{+}
for any
\lambda\in S
. It follows that

\varphi=q^{-\mu_{0}}\sum_{\lambda\in S}\varphi_{\lambda}(q^{\lambda+\mu_{0}}-f_{(w_{P}w_{0})^{-1}(\lambda+\mu_{0}),v_{w_{P}w_{0}}})+q^{-\mu_{0}}\sum_{\lambda\in S}\varphi_{\lambda}f_{(w_{P}w_{0})^{-1}(\lambda+\mu_{0}),v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}.

Put
\phi:=\sum_{\lambda\in S}\varphi_{\lambda}f_{(w_{P}w_{0})^{-1}(\lambda+\mu_{0}),v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}\in\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
. Since
\varphi|_{\mathfrak{X}_{P}}=0
, we have
\phi|_{U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}T^{\vee}U^{\vee}_{-}}=0
. By looking at the line bundles on
G^{\vee}/B^{\vee}_{-}
and using an argument from the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 6.2]
, we see that there exists
\lambda_{0}\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
such that
\phi f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}
belongs to the ideal of
\mathcal{O}(G^{\vee})
generated by
f_{\lambda,v}
with
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
v\in S(\lambda)_{<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)}
. We are done because
\phi=q^{-w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda_{0})}\phi f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{-w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda_{0})}\phi(f_{\lambda_{0},v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda_{0})})
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

(Proposition
7.46
, Lemma
7.47
)
f_{i}-\omega_{i}^{L}f_{0}
and
f_{0}
represent some elements of
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

It is clear that both represent some elements of
\mathcal{K}
and the latter even represents an element of
\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
. It remains to show that
[f_{i}-\omega_{i}^{L}f_{0}]\in\mathcal{K}
is
U^{\vee}_{-}
-invariant, or equivalently
\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
-invariant, modulo
\mathcal{I}^{\mathcal{K}}_{-,\chi}
(see Definition
6.7
). Recall from Example
6.4
we are using the right translation. Let
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
. We have

x\cdot f_{i}=f_{\lambda_{0},x\cdot v_{i}}\quad\text{ and }\quad x\cdot(\omega_{i}^{L}f_{0})=[x,\omega_{i}]^{L}f_{0}=f_{0}([x,\omega_{i}]^{L}-\chi([x,\omega_{i}]))+\chi([x,\omega_{i}])f_{0}.

Hence
x\cdot[f_{i}-\omega_{i}^{L}f_{0}]
is equal to
[f_{\lambda_{0},x\cdot v_{i}}-\chi([x,\omega_{i}])f_{0}]
modulo
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{\mathcal{K}}
.

By
[
3
, Theorem 5.4]
,
[
10
, Lemma A.1]
and a straightforward computation, we have
e_{i}^{\vee}\cdot v_{w_{0}}=-\alpha_{i}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))v_{i}
. (Recall we have made a specific choice of
e_{i}^{\vee}
. See Remark
4.1
.) It follows that

x\cdot v_{i}=-\alpha_{i}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))^{-1}x\cdot(e_{i}^{\vee}\cdot v_{w_{0}})=-\alpha_{i}(w_{0}(\lambda_{0}))^{-1}[x,e_{i}^{\vee}]\cdot v_{w_{0}}.

Notice that
x\in|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\beta(e_{i}^{\vee},x)f_{i}^{\vee}+\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus\{\alpha_{i}\}}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{-\alpha^{\vee}}
. (See the paragraph before Remark
4.1
for the definitions of
|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}
and
\beta(-,-)
.) It follows that

\displaystyle x\cdot v_{i}

\displaystyle=|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\beta(e_{i}^{\vee},x)v_{w_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}|\alpha^{\vee}_{j}|^{2}\beta(e_{j}^{\vee},[x,\omega_{i}])\right)v_{w_{0}}

\displaystyle=\beta(e,[x,\omega_{i}])v_{w_{0}}

\displaystyle=\chi([x,\omega_{i}])v_{w_{0}}.

Therefore,
f_{\lambda_{0},x\cdot v_{i}}-\chi([x,\omega_{i}])f_{0}=0
. The result follows.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

(Lemma
7.47
, Lemma
7.49
) The vector field

\widetilde{\eta}_{i}:=q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\omega_{i}^{L}+\partial_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}(G^{\vee}\times Z(L^{\vee}))

is tangent to
X^{\vee}_{P}
. The restriction

\eta_{i}:=\widetilde{\eta}_{i}|_{X^{\vee}_{P}}\in\mathfrak{X}(X^{\vee}_{P})

is a lift of
\partial_{q_{i}}\in\mathfrak{X}(Z(L^{\vee}))
with respect to
\pi
.

Proof.

The first assertion follows from the observation that the flow of
q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\widetilde{\eta}_{i}
is given by
(g,t)\mapsto(ge^{s\omega_{i}},te^{s\omega_{i}})
. The second assertion is clear from definition.
∎

Lemma A.7
.

(Lemma
7.49
)
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=0
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.11
,
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
is the pull-back of a volume form on
\mathcal{U}_{P}
via
\pr_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\circ\nu
. The flow of
q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\eta_{i}
is given by
(g,t)\mapsto(ge^{s\omega_{i}},te^{s\omega_{i}})
. It is clear that
\pr_{\mathcal{U}_{P}}\circ\nu
is invariant under this flow, and hence
\mathcal{L}_{q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\eta_{i}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=0
. Since
q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}
is a regular function on the base scheme
Z(L^{\vee})
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}=0
.
∎

Lemma A.8
.

(Lemma
7.49
)
q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}f_{i}
is an extension of
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}W
.

Proof.

Let
(g,t)\in X^{\vee}_{P}
be a point where
g=u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tu_{2}
with
u_{1},u_{2}\in U^{\vee}_{-}
.

The flow of
q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\eta_{i}
is given by
(g,t)\mapsto(ge^{s\omega_{i}},te^{s\omega_{i}})
. By the paragraph before Remark
4.1
and a straightforward computation, we see that
\mathcal{L}_{\eta_{i}}W
sends
(g,t)
to
\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(t)^{-1}|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\beta(e_{i}^{\vee},y_{i}(u_{2}))
where
y_{i}:=\pr_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}}}\circ\exp_{U^{\vee}_{-}}^{-1}
is the composition of the inverse of the exponential map
\exp_{U^{\vee}_{-}}:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow U^{\vee}_{-}
and the projection
\pr_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}}}:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{-\alpha^{\vee}_{i}}
.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle q^{-[w_{0}(\lambda_{0})+\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}f_{i}(g,t)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}(w_{0}(t))^{-1}\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(t)^{-1}\langle v_{\lambda_{0}}^{*},u_{1}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}tu_{2}\cdot v_{i}\rangle

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(t)^{-1}\langle v_{\lambda_{0}}^{*},(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}\cdot(y_{i}(u_{2})\cdot v_{i})\rangle.

By the proof of Lemma
A.5
, we have

y_{i}(u_{2})\cdot v_{i}=|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\beta(e_{i}^{\vee},y_{i}(u_{2}))v_{w_{0}}.

By
[
10
, Proposition B.7]
,
(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}\cdot v_{w_{0}}=(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}\cdot v_{w_{P}w_{0}}=v_{e}
. Therefore,

\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(t)^{-1}\langle v_{\lambda_{0}}^{*},(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}\cdot(y_{i}(u_{2})\cdot v_{i})\rangle=\alpha^{\vee}_{i}(t)^{-1}|\alpha^{\vee}_{i}|^{2}\beta(e_{i}^{\vee},y_{i}(u_{2})).

The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

(Lemma
7.49
)
\iota_{\eta_{i}}p^{*}\langle h^{j},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle=q^{-[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\langle h^{j},\omega_{i}\rangle
.

Proof.

The flow of
q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\eta_{i}
is given by
(g,t)\mapsto(ge^{s\omega_{i}},te^{s\omega_{i}})
. Using this flow, it is not hard to see that

\iota_{q^{[\alpha^{\vee}_{i}]}\eta_{i}}p^{*}\langle h^{j},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle=\langle h^{j},\omega_{i}\rangle

The result follows.
∎

Appendix B
Technical results on Brieskorn lattice

We prove three technical results on
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
. They are used in Section
7.7
and rely on some results which are proved prior to that subsection. Denote by
G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)
the fiber of
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
at a given point

(\hslash,h,t)\in\spec\left(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))\right)\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee}).

Proposition B.1
.

For any
(\hslash_{0},h_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee})
and
c\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
\hslash_{0}\neq 0
, we have

\dim G_{0}(\hslash_{0},h_{0},t_{0})=\dim G_{0}(c\hslash_{0},ch_{0},t_{0})<+\infty.

Proof.

We proceed by modifying the proof for the case
h_{0}=0
which is well-known. See e.g.
[
46
, Section 1.1]
where the superpotential is assumed to be projective.

Let
(\hslash,h,t)\in\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\hslash}\times\mathfrak{t}\times Z(L^{\vee})
. Define
X^{\vee}_{t}:=\pi^{-1}(t)
,
W_{t}:=W|_{X^{\vee}_{t}}
,
p_{t}:=p|_{X^{\vee}_{t}}
and
\omega_{h}:=\langle h,\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle
. (Recall
\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}
is the Maurer-Cartan form of
T^{\vee}
.) We have

G_{0}(\hslash,h,t)\simeq\coker\left(\Omega^{top-1}(X^{\vee}_{t})\xrightarrow{~\hslash d+dW_{t}-p_{t}^{*}\omega_{h}~}\Omega^{top}(X^{\vee}_{t})\right).

Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the integrable connection
(\mathcal{O}_{T^{\vee}},d-\omega_{h})
with degree shifted suitably. Then

(B.1)

\mathcal{H}^{0}(\textstyle{\int}_{W_{t}}\circ p_{t}^{{\dagger}})\mathcal{E}\simeq\coker\left(\Omega^{top-1}(X^{\vee}_{t})[\partial_{s}]\xrightarrow{~d-\partial_{s}dW_{t}-p_{t}^{*}\omega_{h}~}\Omega^{top}(X^{\vee}_{t})[\partial_{s}]\right)

where
\int_{W_{t}}
and
p_{t}^{{\dagger}}
are the direct and inverse image functors of
D
-modules respectively. See e.g.
[
19
, Section 1.5]
for more details.

Consider the Fourier transform functor

\mathcal{F}:D_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}}\text{--}\module\rightarrow D_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\tau}}\text{--}\module

defined by sending each
D_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}}
-module to the module with the same underlying vector space on which
\tau
and
\partial_{\tau}
act the same way as
\partial_{s}
and
-s
do respectively. Since
\mathcal{E}
is regular holonomic, so is
\mathcal{H}^{0}(\int_{W_{t}}\circ p_{t}^{{\dagger}})\mathcal{E}
(see e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 6.1.5]
), and hence, by a standard result on differential equations in dimension one (see e.g.
[
47
, Chapter V Proposition 2.2]
), the localization
(\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{H}^{0}(\int_{W_{t}}\circ p_{t}^{{\dagger}})\mathcal{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\tau]}\mathbb{C}[\tau,\tau^{-1}]
is a free
\mathbb{C}[\tau,\tau^{-1}]
-module of finite rank. It follows that, by (
B.1
), the
\mathbb{C}[\tau,\tau^{-1}]
-module

N(h,t):=\coker\left(\Omega^{top-1}(X^{\vee}_{t})[\tau,\tau^{-1}]\xrightarrow{~d-\tau dW_{t}-p_{t}^{*}\omega_{h}~}\Omega^{top}(X^{\vee}_{t})[\tau,\tau^{-1}]\right)

is free of finite rank. Put
(h,t):=(\hslash_{0}^{-1}h_{0},t_{0})
. Then we have

\dim G_{0}(\hslash_{0},h_{0},t_{0})=\dim N(h,t)_{\tau=-\hslash_{0}^{-1}}=\dim N(h,t)_{\tau=-c^{-1}\hslash_{0}^{-1}}=\dim G_{0}(c\hslash_{0},ch_{0},t_{0})<+\infty.

∎

Proposition B.2
.

G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
is
\hslash
-torsion-free.

Proof.

This is proved by Lam and Templier
[
33
, Proposition 16.13]
. For the convenience of the reader, we reproduce their proof in terms of our notations.

Observe that
G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
is the top cohomology of the cochain complex
C^{\bullet}:=(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\Omega^{\bullet}(X^{\vee}_{P}/Z(L^{\vee})),\partial)
where
\partial
is defined similarly as in Definition
4.7
. Since each
C^{i}
is
\hslash
-torsion-free, the sequence

0\rightarrow C^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\hslash}C^{\bullet}\rightarrow C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet}\rightarrow 0

is exact, and hence we have an exact sequence

H^{d-1}(C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet})\rightarrow G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\xrightarrow{\hslash}G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)\rightarrow H^{d}(C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet})\rightarrow 0

where
d
is the top degree. The proof is complete if we can show
H^{d-1}(C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet})=0
.

Take a global frame
\{\zeta_{j}\}
of
X^{\vee}_{P}
relative to
\pi
. For each
j
, define

r_{j}:=1\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\zeta_{j}}W-\sum_{i}h_{i}\otimes\iota_{\zeta_{j}}p^{*}\langle h^{i},\mccccc_{T^{\vee}}\rangle\in R:=\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(X^{\vee}_{P}).

Observe that
C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet}
is the Koszul complex associated to the sequence
\{r_{j}\}
. By the theory of Koszul complexes, the vanishing of
H^{d-1}(C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet})
follows if we can show that
\{r_{j}\}
is an
R_{\mathfrak{p}}
-regular sequence for any prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
R
which contains
\langle r_{j}\rangle
. By a dimension argument, the latter condition holds if we can show that
\spec R/\langle r_{j}\rangle
is quasi-finite over
\spec(\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee})))
. We have

R/\langle r_{j}\rangle\simeq H^{d}(C^{\bullet}/\hslash C^{\bullet})\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)).

By the bijectivity of
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Phi_{i})
for
i=0,1,2
(Proposition
7.24
, Proposition
7.31
and Proposition
7.39
), we have

(B.2)

\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p))\simeq QH_{T}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

which is a finitely generated
\sym^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module. The quasi-finiteness follows.
∎

Remark B.3
.

In the above proof, we deduce the quasi-finiteness from Proposition
7.39
which depends on the
Peterson variety presentation
for
QH_{T}^{\bullet}(G/P)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I\setminus I_{P}]

[
10
, Theorem B]
. It should be pointed out that what we actually need is its corollary: the quasi-finiteness of another scheme
B_{e^{T}}^{\vee}\times_{G^{\vee}}U^{\vee}_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}B^{\vee}_{-}
over the same base scheme, which is proved in the course of the proof of the Peterson variety presentation (see the proof of
[
10
, Lemma 5.7]
). We believe that this less non-trivial result is already known to experts. See e.g. the proof of
[
31
, Proposition 6.2]
.

Proposition B.4
.

G_{0}(X^{\vee}_{P},W,\pi,p)
is a finitely generated
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{0}
is bijective (Proposition
7.24
) and
\Phi_{1}
is surjective (Proposition
7.30
), it suffices to show that
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
is a finitely generated
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module.

Consider first the case
P=B
. By the bijectivity of
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
(Theorem
6.15
) and Proposition
5.12
, the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear map
\Phi_{GMP}^{T}\circ\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is injective and identifies
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
with the submodule

\mathcal{N}:=\bigoplus_{w\in W}\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{w}}H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)\cdot q^{\lambda}\sigma_{w}\subset QH_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(G/B)[q_{i}^{-1}|~i\in I]

where
\Lambda_{w}:=\{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}|~wt_{\lambda}\in W_{af}^{-}\}
. By Lemma
B.5
below, for any
w\in W
, there exist
\lambda_{w,1},\ldots,\lambda_{w,k_{w}}\in Q^{\vee}
such that
\Lambda_{w}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k_{w}}(\lambda_{w,i}-Q^{\vee}_{+})
. Hence
\mathcal{N}
is a finitely generated
H_{\widehat{T}}^{\bullet}(\pt)[-Q^{\vee}_{+}]
-module. Since
\Phi_{BF}^{\delta}
is a homomorphism of modules with respect to the ring isomorphism
\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}
(Theorem
6.15
),
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}_{GMP}^{G}
is a module action (Proposition
5.9
) and
\Phi_{GMP}^{G}\circ\Phi_{BF}^{\kappa}([f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}])=\Phi_{GMP}^{T}([\mathcal{G}r^{\leqslant\lambda}])=q^{w_{0}(\lambda)}
for any
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
(Proposition
5.12
and Proposition
6.17
), it follows that
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
is a finitely generated
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})[Q^{\vee}_{+}]
-module where the
\mathbb{C}[Q^{\vee}_{+}]
-module structure is given by the (right) multiplication by
[f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]\in\kappa_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
. Let
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{N}\}\subset\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})
be a set of generators.

Now let us return to the case where
P
is arbitrary. We will show that
[y_{i}\otimes 1]
,
i=1,\ldots,N
, generate the
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-module
\delta_{\hslash}(\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))/\mathcal{W}
. Every element of this module is a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(Z(L^{\vee}))
-linear combination of
[y_{i}\cdot[f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]\otimes 1]
where
i=1,\ldots,N
and
\lambda\in Q^{\vee}_{+}
. Notice that
[f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]=a_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}
by Lemma
7.9
. If
w_{0}(\lambda)=w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)
, then
a_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}=a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}
, and hence

[y_{i}\cdot[f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]\otimes 1]=[(y_{i}\otimes 1)\cdot(a_{\lambda,v_{w_{P}w_{0}}}-q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]})]+q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}[y_{i}\otimes 1]=q^{[w_{0}(\lambda)]}[y_{i}\otimes 1].

Otherwise, we have
w_{0}(\lambda)<w_{P}w_{0}(\lambda)
, and hence
[y_{i}\cdot[f_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]\otimes 1]=[(y_{i}\otimes 1)\cdot a_{\lambda,v_{w_{0}}}]=0
. The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

For any
w\in W
, there exist
\lambda_{w,1},\ldots,\lambda_{w,k_{w}}\in Q^{\vee}
such that

\Lambda_{w}:=\{\lambda\in Q^{\vee}|~wt_{\lambda}\in W_{af}^{-}\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k_{w}}(\lambda_{w,i}-Q^{\vee}_{+}).

Proof.

First observe that
\Lambda_{w}\subseteq-Q^{\vee}_{+}
and
(-Q^{\vee}_{+})\setminus\Lambda_{w}
is equal to a union of the intersections of
-Q^{\vee}_{+}
and the facets of its convex hull. Embed
Q^{\vee}
into
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
such that
-Q^{\vee}_{+}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{r}\cap Q^{\vee}
. Put
n:=|\mathbb{Z}^{r}/Q^{\vee}|
. Define
S:=[0,n]^{r}\cap\Lambda_{w}
. It is not difficult to see that
\Lambda_{w}=\bigcup_{x\in S}(x-Q^{\vee}_{+})
.
∎

Appendix C
A Remark on Kostant functor

In this appendix, we prove an extension of Lemma
6.9
which is used in Definition
6.16
and the proof of Lemma
7.9
as well as a closely related lemma which is used in the proof of Lemma
6.19
. The key ideas can be found in
[
6
]
and a paper of Kostant
[
28
]
cited therein.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category whose objects are graded left
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-})^{op}
-modules
M
equipped with a linear algebraic
U^{\vee}_{-}
-action satisfying

(1)

\sigma(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}})\cdot M_{i}\subseteq M_{i+2\rho(\alpha^{\vee})}
for any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\sigma:\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}\rightarrow\eendo_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
is the linearization of the
U^{\vee}_{-}
-action and
M_{i}
is the
i
-th graded piece of
M
;

(2)

u\cdot((x\otimes y)\cdot m)=((u\cdot x)\otimes(u\cdot y))\cdot(u\cdot m)
for any
m\in M
,
x\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
,
y\in U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-})
and
u\in U^{\vee}_{-}
; and

(3)

(x\otimes 1-1\otimes x)\cdot m=\hslash\sigma(x)m
for any
m\in M
and
x\in\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-}
,

and whose morphisms are graded
U^{\vee}_{-}
-equivariant
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hslash]}U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{n}^{\vee}_{-})^{op}
-linear maps of degree zero. Clearly, there is a natural functor
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{C}}_{\hslash}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and the functors
\kappa_{\hslash}
,
\delta_{\hslash}
(Definition
6.7
) and the natural transformation
M\mapsto\theta_{M}
(
6.1
) extend to
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}
be the functor
N\mapsto N/\hslash N
. We have a canonical natural transformation

\mathcal{C}\ni M\mapsto\alpha_{M}:\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}\circ\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(M).

Lemma C.1
.

\theta_{M}
is bijective for any
M\in\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma C.2
.

\alpha_{M}
is bijective for any
M\in\mathcal{C}
.

We will prove these lemmas simultaneously. First notice that they hold for any
M\in\mathcal{C}
satisfying
\hslash M=0
. (The bijectivity of
\theta_{M}
follows from Kostant’s slice theorem and descent, and the bijectivity of
\alpha_{M}
is obvious.) For any finite dimensional graded
U^{\vee}_{-}
-module
V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
satisfying
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{\alpha^{\vee}}\cdot V_{i}\subseteq V_{i+2\rho(\alpha^{\vee})}
for any
\alpha\in-R^{+}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, define
\fr(V):=U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})\otimes V
. As in Example
6.2
, we can define an analogous structure on
\fr(V)
so that it is an object of
\mathcal{C}
. Define
\mathcal{C}^{fr}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of objects of this form. Define also

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{+}

\displaystyle:=\{M\in\mathcal{C}|~M[m]\text{ is positively graded for some }m\in\mathbb{Z}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\hslash TF}

\displaystyle:=\{M\in\mathcal{C}|~M\text{ is }\hslash\text{-torsion-free}\}.

Consider the following statements.

(1)

M\in\mathcal{C}^{fr}

\Longrightarrow

\theta_{M}
is bijective.

(2)

M\in\mathcal{C}

\Longrightarrow

\theta_{M}
is bijective.

(3)

M\in\mathcal{C}^{fr}

\Longrightarrow

\alpha_{M}
is bijective.

(4)

M\in\mathcal{C}

\Longrightarrow

\alpha_{M}
is bijective.

(5)

M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
,
M^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\hslash TF}
,
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
exact
\Longrightarrow

\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
exact.

(6)

M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
,
\hslash M^{\prime}=0
,
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
exact
\Longrightarrow

\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
exact.

(7)

M,M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
,
M\rightarrow M^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
exact
\Longrightarrow

\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
exact.

(Notice that if
N\in\mathcal{C}
, then
N/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}\in\mathcal{C}
.) We are going to verify (1) and the implications

(1)\Rightarrow(3)\Rightarrow(5)\wedge(6)\Rightarrow(7)\quad\text{ and }\quad(1)\wedge(7)\Rightarrow(2)\Rightarrow(4).

This will complete the proof of Lemma
C.1
and Lemma
C.2
because they correspond to (2) and (4) respectively.

(1) is true.
Let
M:=\fr(V)\in\mathcal{C}^{fr}
. Since
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
is a free
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module of rank
|W|
, we can write
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
as
(\kappa_{\hslash}(M))^{\oplus|W|}
. We have the commutative diagram

Observe that
M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}
is
\hslash
-torsion-free. It follows that
\alpha_{M}
is injective and so is
\alpha_{M}^{\oplus|W|}
. Thus
\theta_{M}
is injective by a Nakayama-type argument.

As for the surjectivity, consider the canonical filtration on
V
by degrees which induces a filtration on
M
by sub-objects, and then a filtration on
M/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}
by sub-
U^{\vee}_{-}
-modules whose associated graded pieces are direct sums of finitely many copies of
Y_{\hslash,\chi}:=\fr(\mathbb{C})/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}
.
Using the long exact sequences for group cohomology, five lemma and induction, it suffices to show that (i)
\theta_{\fr(\mathbb{C})}
is surjective (we have proved that
\theta_{\fr(\mathbb{C})}
is injective) and (ii)
H^{1}(U^{\vee}_{-};Y_{\hslash,\chi})=0
.

(i) is obvious because
\delta_{\hslash}(\fr(\mathbb{C}))\simeq U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
. To prove (ii), it suffices to verify

H^{1}(U^{\vee}_{-};U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}Y_{\hslash,\chi})=0

because
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
is free over
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
(here
U^{\vee}_{-}
acts trivially on
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
). We have the action morphism
Y_{\hslash,\chi}\rightarrow Y_{\hslash,\chi}\otimes\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-})
. Composing it with the quotient map
Y_{\hslash,\chi}\rightarrow Y_{\hslash,\chi}/\mathcal{I}_{+}\simeq U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
yields a map
Y_{\hslash,\chi}\rightarrow U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-})
. Observe that the last map is
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-linear if we twist the target
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-module by
\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}
, and hence by extension of scalars we obtain a
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
-linear map

\Theta:U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}Y_{\hslash,\chi}\rightarrow\twist_{-\rho^{\vee}}(U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}))\otimes\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-}).

It is not difficult to see that
\Theta
is graded and
U^{\vee}_{-}
-linear where the
U^{\vee}_{-}
-action on
\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-})
is induced by right translation. Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Theta)
is induced by the canonical section
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}\times_{\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}/W}(e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-})
. Since
e+\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}_{-}
is a
U^{\vee}_{-}
-torsor over
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}/W
by Kostant’s slice theorem,
\mathcal{F}_{\hslash=0}(\Theta)
is bijective. By a Nakayama-type argument,
\Theta
is bijective as well. Now the vanishing of
H^{1}(U^{\vee}_{-};U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}Y_{\hslash,\chi})
follows from the vanishing of
H^{1}(U^{\vee}_{-};\mathcal{O}(U^{\vee}_{-}))
. A proof of the latter result can be found in the proof of
[
28
, Lemma 4.2]
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3) and (2)
\Rightarrow
(4).
Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
. Suppose
\theta_{M}
is bijective. Since
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})
is a free
Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})
-module of rank
|W|
, we can write
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
as
(\kappa_{\hslash}(M))^{\oplus|W|}
. We have the commutative diagram

\hbox to290.67pt{\vbox to77.29pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 46.30415pt\lower-65.23853pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-42.97115pt}{-3.18945pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{71}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

The bottom and right arrows are always bijective. By assumption, the left arrow is bijective. It follows that
\alpha_{M}^{\oplus|W|}
, and hence
\alpha_{M}
, is bijective.

\wedge
(6).
Suppose the conditions in (5) are satisfied. We may assume
M
is a (possibly infinite) direct sum of objects of
\mathcal{C}^{fr}
because every object of
\mathcal{C}
is a quotient of such an object. We have the commutative diagram

By Kostant’s slice theorem and descent, the rightmost arrow is surjective. Since
M^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}\in\mathcal{C}_{\hslash TF}
,
\alpha_{M^{\prime}}
is injective. By (3),
\alpha_{M}
is surjective. Hence we can apply a Nakayama-type argument to get the surjectivity of
\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})
. (The condition
M^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
guarantees that the inductive process will terminate.)

Now suppose the conditions in (6) are satisfied. Again, we assume
M
is a direct sum of objects of
\mathcal{C}^{fr}
so that
\alpha_{M}
is surjective by (3). Consider the above diagram. By
\hslash M^{\prime}=0
, the two arrows in the bottom row are bijective. This yields the surjectivity of
\kappa_{\hslash}(M)\rightarrow\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})
immediately.

(5)
\wedge
(6)
\Rightarrow
(7).
Let
f:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
denote the given surjective map. We first show that we can assume
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}}=0
and
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
. Since
\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})\simeq\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}})
, we can replace
M^{\prime}
by
M^{\prime}/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}}
so that
M^{\prime}
now satisfies
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}}=0
. Let
y\in\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})=(M^{\prime})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
. There exists
g:\fr(V)\rightarrow M^{\prime}
such that
M^{\prime\prime}:=\im(g)
contains
y
. Since
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}}=0
,
g
factors through
\fr(V)/\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{\fr(V)}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
, and hence
M^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
. Then we can replace
f
by
f|_{f^{-1}(M^{\prime\prime})}:f^{-1}(M^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow M^{\prime\prime}
.

From now on, we do assume
\mathcal{I}_{-,\chi}^{M^{\prime}}=0
and
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{+}
. For each
i\geqslant 0
, define
M^{\prime}_{i}:=\ker(\hslash^{i}:M^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime})
. Define also
M^{\prime}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}^{\infty}M^{\prime}_{i}
. Let
z\in\kappa_{\hslash}(M^{\prime})=(M^{\prime})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
. By applying (5) to the composition
M\xrightarrow{f}M^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}/M^{\prime}_{\infty}
, we obtain
x_{0}\in\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
such that
z-\kappa_{\hslash}(f)(x_{0})\in(M_{k}^{\prime})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
for some
k
. By applying (6) to the composition
f^{-1}(M_{k}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{f|_{f^{-1}(M_{k}^{\prime})}}M_{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow M_{k}^{\prime}/M_{k-1}^{\prime}
, we obtain
x_{1}\in\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
such that
z-\kappa_{\hslash}(f)(x_{0}+x_{1})\in(M_{k-1}^{\prime})^{U^{\vee}_{-}}
. Continuing, we get
x_{2},\ldots,x_{k}
such that
z-\kappa_{\hslash}(f)(x_{0}+\cdots+x_{k})\in M_{0}^{\prime}=\{0\}
, i.e.
z=\kappa_{\hslash}(f)(x_{0}+\cdots+x_{k})
.

(1)
\wedge
(7)
\Rightarrow
(2).
Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
. There exists an exact sequence
L_{1}\rightarrow L_{2}\rightarrow M\rightarrow 0
where
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are (possibly infinite) direct sums of objects of
\mathcal{C}^{fr}
. We have the commutative diagram

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{273.33208pt}{-27.37335pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{71}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

By (1),
\theta_{L_{1}}
and
\theta_{L_{2}}
are bijective. By (7), the sequence in the top row is exact at
U_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{t}^{\vee})\otimes_{Z_{\hslash}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}\kappa_{\hslash}(M)
. Since
\delta_{\hslash}
is right exact, the sequence in the bottom row is exact. It follows that
\theta_{M}
is bijective by the five lemma.

Appendix D
Rietsch mirror: Lam-Templier’s vs ours

We show that our version of the Rietsch mirror (Section
4.1
) is equivalent to the one defined by Lam and Templier
[
33
, Section 6.4]
. As explained in
loc. cit.
, the latter is equivalent to Rietsch’s original one
[
45
, Section 4]
.

First, we interchange the roles of
G
and
G^{\vee}
. In other words,
G
(resp.
G^{\vee}
) is now denoted by
G^{\vee}
(resp.
G
). We also relabel the related objects (e.g. subgroups, roots, etc) correspondingly. In particular, we now have

X^{\vee}_{P}\simeq U_{-}(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}Z(L)U_{-}\cap B.

In
[
33
, Section 6.4]
, Lam and Templier defined the Rietsch mirror to be

X_{LT}:=UZ(L)\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}U\cap B_{-}

where
\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}\in N(T)
(which is
\dot{w}_{P}
in
loc. cit.
) is a representative of
w_{P}w_{0}
, defined using

\dot{s}_{\alpha_{i},LT}:=x_{i}(-1)y_{i}(1)x_{i}(-1)

(see
[
33
, Section 2.2]
) instead of
\dot{s}_{\alpha_{i}}=y_{i}(-1)x_{i}(1)y_{i}(-1)
which is what we are using. The analogues of
W
,
\pi
and
p
are defined in
[
33
, Section 6.4]
; the analogue of
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
is defined in
[
33
, Section 6.6]
; and the analogue of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action is defined in
[
33
, Section 6.21]
.

Let
g\mapsto g^{T}
be the unique involutive antiautomorphism of
G
satisfying

t^{T}=t,~t\in T\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{i}(a)^{T}=y_{i}(a),~a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}.

Define
g^{-T}:=(g^{-1})^{T}=(g^{T})^{-1}
.

Lemma D.1
.

The map
g\mapsto g^{T}
induces an isomorphism

X^{\vee}_{P}\simeq X_{LT}.

It preserves the additional structures including
W
,
\pi
,
p
,
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
and the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action.

Proof.

To prove the first part, it suffices to show
\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}=(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-T}
. Observe that
\dot{s}_{\alpha_{i},LT}=(\dot{s}_{\alpha_{i}})^{T}
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
. It follows that
\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}=\dot{(w_{0}w_{P})}^{T}
. Since
\ell(w_{0}w_{P})+\ell(w_{P})=\ell(w_{0})
, we have
\dot{(w_{0}w_{P})}\dot{w}_{P}=\dot{w}_{0}
, and hence
\dot{(w_{0}w_{P})}=\dot{w}_{0}\dot{w}_{P}^{-1}
. But
\dot{w}_{0}=\dot{w}_{0}^{-1}
because
G
(which was
G^{\vee}
originally) is of adjoint type. It follows that
\dot{(w_{0}w_{P})}=\dot{w}_{0}^{-1}\dot{w}_{P}^{-1}=(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-1}
, and hence
\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}=\dot{(w_{0}w_{P})}^{T}=(\dot{w}_{P}\dot{w}_{0})^{-T}
as desired.

It remains to show that the above isomorphism preserves the additional structures. Let us handle the fiberwise volume forms only. The others are straightforward and left to the reader. Recall
\omega_{X^{\vee}_{P}}
is defined to be the pullback of a volume form on
\mathcal{U}_{P}
via the isomorphism
\nu:X^{\vee}_{P}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{U}_{P}\times Z(L)
. See Definition
4.11
. The analogue is the isomorphism
\nu_{LT}:X_{LT}\xrightarrow{\sim}\overset{\circ}{G/P}\times Z(L)
defined by

\nu_{LT}(u_{1}t\dot{(w_{P}w_{0})}_{LT}u_{2}):=(u_{2}^{-1}\dot{w}_{0}P,t)\quad\text{ assuming }~u_{1}\in U\cap\dot{w}_{P}U\dot{w}_{P}^{-1},

where
\overset{\circ}{G/P}
is the isomorphic image of the open Richardson variety
\mathcal{R}^{w_{0}}_{w_{P}}:=B_{-}\dot{w}_{P}B/B\cap B\dot{w}_{0}B/B\subseteq G/B
under the projection
G/B\rightarrow G/P
. See
[
33
, Section 6.6]
. Consider the diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

It is not difficult to show that the bottom arrow is an isomorphism. By
[
33
, Lemma 6.7]
, this diagram (without the middle arrow) is commutative. This induces the middle arrow which is also an isomorphism such that the whole diagram is commutative. Since the volume forms from both definitions are uniquely determined (up to scalar) by the corresponding boundary divisors, we are done.
∎
